Aevia Language Specification

Aevia — Design Standard

Aevia is a pure functional language for scientific computing in which dimensions are part of the type system. A quantity is not a number with a run-time tag; it is a value whose type records its physical dimension. The compiler rejects 3 m + 2 s, sin(3 kg) and exp(1 m) before any code runs.

The language is built on the rssn computer-algebra and scientific-computing engine. Symbolic and numeric evaluation are two phases of the same expression; units, dimensional inference, rewriting, numeric kernels, JIT compilation and physics simulation are provided by the backend. Aevia keeps no second operator list or unit table: it consumes rssn's Kernel Manifest and rssn::dim registry and generates its Rssn module from them. See 13 — Extensibility and rssn's docs/KERNEL.md.

This document is the normative design: the grammar is complete, the typing rules are stated, the run-time architecture is fixed, and every backend dependency is identified. Examples and rationale are informative.

The backend interface contract is in the rssn repository's docs/KERNEL.md (Kernel Manifest, dynamic-call ABI, plugins, units and dimensions) and docs/DIMENSIONS.md; it is a precondition of this specification, not an optional implementation detail. The division of the implementation into crates is in ARCHITECTURE.md at the repository root. Every chapter also has a Chinese edition (the source text); authoring and terminology rules are in spec/STYLE.md.

Conformance language

The key words MUST, MUST NOT, REQUIRED, SHOULD, SHOULD NOT and MAY are to be interpreted as described in RFC 2119. A conforming implementation MUST accept every program in this specification's test corpus, MUST reject every program marked ill-typed, and MUST produce the specified diagnostic category (the exact wording is not normative).

Design status

This is a design standard, not a frozen implementation. Chapters marked normative are intended to be implemented as written; appendix C records every revision and the known gaps. The current text is Revision 23, aligned with rssn 3.0.0-rc.1 (api_version = 2). The size of the manifest is a measurement, not a constant; the live output of aevia rssn is authoritative (at that version: 1620 operators, 82 units, 30 constants). Dispatch and extension follow the boundary stated in chapter 13: no run-time multimethods, no multi-parameter classes, no per-type monomorphisation; instances are selected statically or by canonical witnesses.

There is no v1/v2 language split: every capability is designed in; a backend that does not yet implement one reports a capability diagnostic (E1001) instead of removing the language feature.

A one-sentence summary

Aevia makes the dimension of a quantity a type, the unit a compile-time scale, the formula a first-class value, and the numeric result a deliberate request.

Quick taste

module Kinematics

open Units.SI
import Math { sqrt }

let g = 9.80665 m/s^2

fn fall_time(h: Float[L]): Float[T] =
    sqrt(2 * h / g)

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    let h = 20.0 m
    println("fall time from {h} = {fall_time(h)}")
$ aevia run kinematics.aev
fall time from 20.0 m = 2.0196199771025523 s

The next chapter explains why this program type-checks, and what the language does when it does not.

00 — Overview: what Aevia is and why it is built this way

0.1 Definition

Aevia is a pure functional language for scientific computing. Dimensions are part of the type, units are compile-time scales, formulas are first-class values, and a numeric result is a deliberate request for a different phase.

It targets one kind of program: start from a physical law, a set of measurements or a differential equation; derive symbolically, check dimensions, discretise numerically, visualise; end with a reproducible result. Such programs are usually assembled from Python, NumPy, SymPy and Pint: dimension checking lives in a library, symbolic and numeric work use two APIs, and unit errors tend to surface only at run time. Aevia puts these in one language and lets the compiler reject dimension errors at the cheapest moment — type checking.

module Kinematics

open Units.SI
import Math { sqrt }

let g = 9.80665 m/s^2

fn fall_time(h: Float[L]): Float[T] =
    sqrt(2 * h / g)

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    let h = 20.0 m
    println("fall time from {h} = {fall_time(h)}")

There are no casts, unit strings or Quantity wrappers. h : Float[L] and g : Real[L/T^2]; dimension algebra gives 2 * h / g the dimension T^2, and sqrt halves the exponent, yielding Float[T]. Write h + g and the program does not run.

0.2 Goals

In priority order:

  1. Dimensional correctness by construction. Addition, subtraction and comparison require equal dimensions; multiplication, division and powers combine them by dimension algebra; transcendental functions require dimensionless arguments. A dimension error is a type error, not a run-time warning.
  2. Symbolic and numeric work are two phases of one language. The same expression can be simplified to closed form or evaluated at a point.
  3. Code that reads like mathematics. Unit literals such as 9.81 m/s^2, Unicode identifiers such as Δt, infix operators, the pipe |>, pattern matching.
  4. A pure core with explicit effects. No mutable state, no implicit IO. Effects are confined to IO, so pure functions can be parallelised, cached, differentiated and substituted freely.
  5. Predictable performance. Strict evaluation; a tensor's rank is in its type and its shape is known at run time; numeric hot spots go down to rssn's kernels and JIT; symbolic search budgets are explicit.
  6. A small core and a thick standard library. The language has functions, algebraic data types, records, type classes, modules and IO; mathematics, units, linear algebra, statistics, ODE/PDE and plotting live in the library.
  7. Errors that teach. A dimension error names the dimensions on both sides and suggests a likely fix (appendix B).

0.3 Non-goals

  • Not a systems language. No manual memory management, no unsafe, no pointer arithmetic; low-level performance comes from rssn's kernels.
  • Not a proof assistant. Dimension types are equations over an abelian group, not full dependent types. Array shapes enter the type as refinements, and type-level Nat supports linear arithmetic only (§13.9.1).
  • No attempt at a Python-sized ecosystem. Data is exchanged through CSV/NPY/JSON, the C ABI, plugins and the registry; Python is not embedded.
  • No user-defined syntax, macros or effect handlers. The extension points are RSSN operators, plugins, Rssn dynamic calls and the package registry, which keeps tooling, formatting and error messages uniform (§13.9.6, §13.9.7).
  • No automatic parallelism. Parallelism must be requested explicitly with primitives such as par_map; devices, GPUs and distribution are run-time targets, not type parameters (§13.9.4).

0.4 Design principles

1. Dimensions are types; units are not

  • A dimension lives in the type: L, T, M*L^2/T^2. It decides which operations a value may take part in.
  • A unit is a compile-time constant on the value side: m, km, degC. It supplies a scale (and an affine offset) and is folded away during compilation.

So 3 km and 3000 m have the same type, Real[L], and are the same value at run time; to(km, x) changes only the display unit. Type equality is therefore dimension equality, there is no combinatorial explosion from "metres and centimetres are different types", and the unit table can grow without touching the type checker. The price is that the type system cannot tell a length in metres from a length in feet. This is deliberate: errors in scientific code are almost always dimension errors, not unit choices within one dimension.

2. Exact and approximate are two types, not two modes

Type Meaning
Int arbitrary-precision integer, dimensionless
Real[d] exact real or symbolic expression of dimension d: 1/3, sqrt(2), x^2 + 1
Float[d] IEEE-754 binary64 of dimension d: 1.5
Complex[d] complex scalar

Integer literals are Int, decimal literals are Float, and 1/10 is an exact Real. In mixed arithmetic Real absorbs Float (exact wins) with warning W0302: 1/3 + 0.5 is 5/6. The language never converts Real to Float where you did not write Float, so sin(1) stays symbolic as sin(1) while sin(1.0) yields 0.8414709848078965. The author must say whether they are deriving exactly or computing numerically (rationale: §12.8.9).

3. Pure, but allowed to look imperative

There are no mutable variables, no for loops and no in-place updates. Iteration is recursion with guaranteed tail calls; array updates are copies semantically and happen in place at run time when ownership is unique. do blocks serve IO only; they are not general monad syntax.

4. Symbolic computation is a value, not a macro

There is no quote/unquote. symbol("x") returns a Real, and everything you can do to it — add, multiply, differentiate, substitute — is what you can do to any Real. diff(fn x => x^2)(3 m) has type Real[L], because differentiation divides by the dimension of the variable. "Derive the formula, then substitute data" is the default workflow.

5. Yield on convenience, never on soundness

Aevia uses Hindley–Milner inference with type classes and dimension algebra rather than full dependent types. Users need not annotate mean(xs), yet can know its dimension without running anything; dimension equations are linear over an abelian group, so inference is decidable and linear. Where static types cannot express a program (say x^n with a run-time integer n), the language does not invent an unsound type: it offers the explicit dynamic escape Dyn, deferring the check to run time. The static path stays sound; the dynamic path stays usable.

6. One registry, reflection first

Aevia does not hand-write RSSN operator bindings or keep a second unit table. OpTable is the only operator registry and rssn::dim the only dimension/unit layer; Aevia reads the Kernel Manifest and generates the Rssn module (typed plus dynamic). New operators, units and plugins need no compiler change.

0.5 A ten-minute tour

Every example below runs; the outputs were checked against the implementation.

Dimensions and units

let d = 100 m                 # Real[L]
let t = 9.58 s                # Real[T]
let v = d / t                 # Real[L/T]
let E = 70 kg * v^2 / 2       # Real[M*L^2/T^2]

let bad = d + t               # error[E0401]: dimension mismatch

Names such as Energy are only print aliases for M*L^2/T^2, not new types.

Dimension polymorphism

fn mean<d>(xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>): Real[d] =
    sum(xs) / length(xs)

fn variance<d>(xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>): Real[d^2] =
    let m = mean(xs)
    sum((xs - m)^2) / length(xs)

mean works for any dimension, and the dimension of variance is the square of the input's. Dimensions are erased at run time; no code is generated per dimension.

Symbolic and numeric

let x = symbol("x")                   # Real, dimensionless by default
let df = diff(fn t => t^3 - 3*t + 1)  # the derivative, as a function
let slope = df(x)                     # 3*x^2 - 3
let y = approx(df(2))                 # Ok(9.0)

diff takes a function and returns its derivative: internally it creates a symbol of the same dimension, applies the function, differentiates and substitutes back, so it works with higher-order functions, partial application and user-defined functions alike.

Tensors

let M = [[1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]]
let b = [5.0, 6.0]
let x = match linsolve(M, b) with
        | Ok(v) -> v
        | Err(_) -> [0.0, 0.0]        # illustration; handle the error in real code
let y = M @ x                         # ≈ b

let R = [[1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]] * ohm
let I = [0.1, 0.2] * A
let V = R @ I                         # [0.5 V, 1.1 V]

@ is matrix multiplication and * is element-wise (scalars broadcast), as in NumPy.

Variants and pattern matching

type Shape =
    | Circle(Real[L])
    | Rect(Real[L], Real[L])

fn area(s: Shape): Real[L^2] =
    match s with
    | Circle(r) -> pi * r^2
    | Rect(w, h) -> w * h

A pure core and IO

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    r <- read_csv<{ time: Float[T], velocity: Float[L/T] }>("data.csv")
    match r with
    | Err(e) -> println("cannot read: {e}")
    | Ok(xs) ->
        match (column(xs, "time"), column(xs, "velocity")) with
        | (Ok(t), Ok(v)) ->
            println("mean speed = {mean(v)}")
            plot(combine([label(line(t, v), "v(t)")]))
        | _ -> println("column type mismatch")

read_csv, plot and println are IO actions; mean is a pure function; main is the only entry point the runtime calls.

0.6 Language and backend layers

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Aevia source (.aev)                                          │
│  lex / layout → syntax tree → name resolution →              │
│  type + dimension checking → core IR                         │
└───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘
                            │ pure values, closures, tensors, symbolic scalars
┌───────────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────────┐
│ Aevia runtime                                                │
│  interpreter · tensor runtime · symbolic bridge ·            │
│  budget/cache · REPL · LSP                                   │
└───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┘
                            │ Term / Config / compute / kernels
┌───────────────────────────▼──────────────────────────────────┐
│ rssn                                                         │
│  e-graph rewriting · dimension inference · numeric kernels · │
│  JIT · simulation scenarios                                  │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Aevia owns syntax, type and dimension checking, closures and records, tensor shapes, modules, IO and the orchestration of the standard library. rssn owns the representation and rewriting of symbolic expressions, the run-time part of dimension algebra, numeric algorithms, linear algebra, FFT, ODE/PDE, special functions, the JIT and physical simulation. The boundary is the Term: a Real or Complex scalar that needs symbolic power holds an rssn term, while Float scalars and numeric tensors take a native fast path and never enter the term graph.

The language's dimension check is compile-time; rssn's dimension layer is run-time and term-level. They share one set of base dimensions and one exponent algebra: the former rejects bad programs, the latter keeps simplification correct and reports diagnostics. For what rssn provides and how it is called, see chapters 10 and 13.

0.7 Conventions

  • Blocks tagged aevia are Aevia source; blocks tagged text are grammar, derivations or output.
  • Typing judgements are written e : τ; dimensions are d, e; the type environment is Γ. In Real[d], d is a dimension expression; <d> declares a dimension parameter.
  • The words must / must not / should / may carry the force of RFC 2119 MUST / MUST NOT / SHOULD / MAY (see the README).
  • ... in an example means elision, not valid syntax.

The next chapter starts at the bottom: the characters, tokens and layout rules that make up an Aevia source file.

01 — Lexical structure

This chapter defines how a source file is split into tokens. Syntax is chapter 02. The semantics of unit literals is chapter 05; this chapter defines only how they are recognised.

1.1 Source files and encoding

  • The extension is .aev; the encoding is UTF-8. A BOM is allowed and ignored.
  • \n, \r\n and \r are all normalised to \n before tokenising.
  • Identifiers (including keywords and unit names) are compared in Unicode NFC: a precomposed é and e plus a combining accent are the same name. Strings and comments are kept as written.
  • The first line may be a shebang (#! to end of line); it is ignored.
  • A tab MUST NOT appear in leading indentation (E0101). Tabs in strings and comments are unrestricted.

1.2 Tokens

token ::= IDENT | UIDENT | KEYWORD | INT | FLOAT | STRING
        | UNIT_NAME | SUPERSCRIPT | LT_APP | ELLIPSIS
        | OP | LPAREN | RPAREN | LBRACKET | RBRACKET | LBRACE | RBRACE
        | COMMA | SEMICOLON | COLON | DOT | DOTDOT | DOTDOTEQ
        | ARROW | FATARROW | LARROW | PIPE | EQUALS
        | NEWLINE | INDENT | DEDENT | EOF

Whitespace separates tokens, and newlines also take part in layout (§1.4). Apart from unit literals there is no implicit multiplication by juxtaposition: 3 x is not a product and f x is not a call. Calls are written f(x) and products 3 * x.

1.3 Comments

# line comment
let x = 1  # trailing comment

#- block comment
   #- may nest -#
   still inside -#
  • # starts a line comment. #- … -# is a nestable block comment; an unclosed one is E0104.
  • ## starts a doc comment (Markdown). It must begin a line (indentation allowed) and attaches to the next declaration.
  • Comments produce no tokens.

1.4 Layout

Aevia uses the offside rule: blocks are delimited by indentation alone; there is no end.

1.4.1 INDENT / DEDENT

The lexer keeps an indentation stack, initially [0]. For each logical line (outside brackets, not blank, not comment-only):

  1. Count the leading spaces n.
  2. n > top: push n and emit INDENT.
  3. n == top: emit nothing.
  4. n < top: pop, emitting one DEDENT per pop, until top == n; if the stack empties first, report E0102.
  5. Emit NEWLINE at the end of the line.

Blank and comment-only lines do not affect indentation and emit no NEWLINE.

1.4.2 Free layout inside brackets

Inside ( [ { no NEWLINE/INDENT/DEDENT is produced, and a trailing comma is allowed:

let A = [[1.0, 2.0],
         [3.0, 4.0]]

let B = [
    1.0, 2.0,
    3.0, 4.0,
]

1.4.3 Continuation

Outside brackets nothing suppresses the line end: every line ends with NEWLINE, and a trailing + does not join the next line (1 + followed by a newline is E0202). A multi-line expression MUST be parenthesised:

let energy = (m * c^2
            + 1/2 * m * v^2
            - m * g * h)

When a line ends with a block-introducing token (=, then, else, ->, =>, do, with) and the next line is indented, the parser reads NEWLINE INDENT expr DEDENT as a layout group. Layout groups are used for function and let bodies, if branches, match arm bodies and do statement bodies. This keeps the lexer ignorant of which tokens are operators.

1.4.4 Blocks

A block is also NEWLINE INDENT … DEDENT, containing a sequence of let/fn declarations and a final expression; its value is the final expression's value, and a block of only lets is E0201. After NEWLINE INDENT the parser chooses a block if the first token is let or fn, and a layout group otherwise:

fn mean<d>(xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>): Real[d] =
    let n = length(xs)
    sum(xs) / n

A position that requires an indented block but has none is E0103. In a multi-line if, else MUST be in the same column as if. The | arms of one match MUST share a column, either the column of match or one level deeper (§2.3.4).

1.5 Identifiers and keywords

1.5.1 Identifiers

ident_start    ::= XID_Start | "_"
ident_continue ::= XID_Continue | "'" | "₀".."₉"
  • Unicode letters (Δt, λ), primes (f') and subscript digits (x₁) are allowed; names are case-sensitive.
  • A name starting with an ASCII capital is a UIDENT (types, constructors, modules, dimensions); all others are IDENT. So Δt is a value name, not a constructor.
  • A lone _ is the wildcard. As a call argument it is a placeholder (§2.3.6). It is not a binding name.

1.5.2 Keywords

as       class     derive    dimension  do       else     false
fn       for       if        import     in       instance let
match    module    open      private    rec      test     then
true     type      unit      where      with

A keyword cannot be used as an identifier (E0206). forall is a contextual keyword: it is a quantifier only at the start of a type, followed by type parameters and ., and it is also a standard-library function name (§8.13). Int, Real, Float, Bool and the like are treated specially only in type position; they are not keywords. in belongs to let … in, for to instance … for and derive … for, and rec to let rec. pure is a Prelude function, not a keyword (§9.7.3).

Status: backtick identifiers (naming an external item that collides with a keyword) are not implemented; a backtick is E0105.

1.5.3 Naming conventions (informative)

Types, constructors, modules and dimensions use UpperCamelCase; functions, values and fields use snake_case; type parameters are lower-case a b, dimension parameters d e; unit names keep their international spelling (m, kg, Pa, °C).

1.6 Numeric literals

int_lit   ::= dec_int | "0x" hexdigit {["_"] hexdigit}
            | "0o" octdigit {["_"] octdigit} | "0b" bindigit {["_"] bindigit}
dec_int   ::= digit { ["_"] digit }
float_lit ::= dec_int "." dec_int [ exponent ] | dec_int exponent
exponent  ::= ("e" | "E") ["+" | "-"] dec_int
  • Underscores only group digits; they cannot lead, trail or repeat (E0106). 1_000 is valid; 1__0 and 1_ are not.
  • A decimal integer is an Int; a literal with a point or exponent is a Float. 0755 is decimal 755. There are no numeric suffixes, and 1. and .5 are invalid.
  • oo, -oo and nan are predefined constants, not literals. i is the predefined imaginary unit; write a complex number as 3 + 4 * i.
  • 0 is not dimension-polymorphic: it defaults to Int. Write zero metres as 0 m and a floating zero as 0.0 (§4.7).

1.7 String literals

"hello"
"value = {x + 1}"
"line\nbreak"
r"C:\data\raw.csv"          # raw string: no escapes, no interpolation
"""
multi-line string
  keeps relative indentation
"""
  • Strings are delimited by double quotes; a single quote is not a string delimiter (E0105).
  • Escapes: \n \t \r \\ \" \{ \} \u{1F600}. An unknown escape is E0105.
  • Interpolation {expr}: the expression's type MUST implement Show. An empty {} is E0104. A literal brace is written \{.
  • A raw string r"…" processes neither backslashes nor braces and cannot contain ".
  • A multi-line string """…""" drops the leading newline and the common indentation of its lines; interior newlines are kept.
  • A string is an immutable UTF-8 sequence; indexing counts grapheme clusters (§8.1.4).

1.8 Unit literals

9.80665 m/s^2
3.0e8 m/s
1.5 kg*m^2/s^3
25 °C
50 %
2.5 µm

1.8.1 Recognition

An identifier sequence immediately after a numeric literal is always a unit literal, never a product.

3 m          # a quantity: 3 metres
3 * m        # the value 3 times the unit value m; display unit may differ
3 x          # a unit literal; E0501 if x is not a unit

The rule is context-free: neither lexer nor parser needs to know which names are units. Name resolution then checks that x lives in the unit namespace, so unit declarations may come later and the parser never backtracks.

quantity_lit ::= number unit_expr
unit_expr    ::= unit_factor { ("*" | "·" | "/") unit_factor }
unit_factor  ::= unit_atom [ "^" exponent | SUPERSCRIPT+ ]
unit_atom    ::= unit_name | "(" unit_expr ")" | "%"
unit_name    ::= UNIT_NAME | IDENT | qname
exponent     ::= ["-"] integer | "(" rational ")"
  • number is an INT or FLOAT of §1.6. A unit literal starts when the numeric literal is immediately followed by an IDENT, a UNIT_NAME (°C), % or (.
  • * and · are synonyms; / is left-associative: kg*m^2/s^3 = (kg*m^2)/s^3.
  • An exponent MUST be an integer literal or a parenthesised rational: m^2, s^-1, m^(1/2). Variable exponents are not allowed (use Dyn, §4.9). Superscripts m², s⁻¹, m³·s⁻² are equivalent to ^n (E0107: invalid sequence).
  • Juxtaposition inside a unit expression is not allowed: kg m^2 is not kg*m^2.
  • Inside a unit expression, * · / MUST touch both neighbours. A * or / with whitespace on either side ends the unit literal and is ordinary arithmetic: 9.81 m/s^2 * mass = (9.81 m/s^2) * mass; 70 kg * v^2 / 2 = ((70 kg) * v^2) / 2; 3 m * x = (3 m) * x. The tight 3 m*x is still a unit expression and is E0501 when x is not a unit. The formatter always prints units tight and arithmetic operators spaced, so formatting never changes meaning.
  • Numbers are not allowed inside a unit expression: 3 m/2 s is (3 m) / (2 s); 1/2 m is 1 / (2 m) with type Real[1/L]; write "half a metre" as (1/2) m.
  • Parentheses are for units only: 3 (m/s) is valid; 3 (2) is E0108; a malformed unit in parentheses such as 3 () is E0209.

1.8.2 Unit names and qualification

  • Unit names are resolved in the unit namespace, separate from values: let s = 5 does not affect 3 s; to multiply by the variable write 3 * s.
  • A name may carry a prefix (km, MHz, µs), matched longest-first (§5.3), and is case-sensitive (Pa ≠ pa).
  • A name may be qualified: 3 Units.SI.km is valid; 3 p.x is not (p is not a unit).
  • % is a unit, not a modulo operator: 50% and 50 % are both the Real[1] value 0.5; 50 % 3 is a syntax error. Use mod(a, b) or rem(a, b) for remainders.

1.8.3 Superscripts and special characters

The superscript characters ⁰¹²³⁴⁵⁶⁷⁸⁹ ⁻ ⁺ may only follow a unit name; x² is not an expression — write x^2.

Written Meaning
°C °F Celsius, Fahrenheit (affine, §5.6)
° alone the angle unit deg
Ω / Å ohm / angstrom
µ (U+00B5), μ (U+03BC) both are the micro prefix, the same prefix

1.8.4 Type and value (preview)

Literal Default type Value
3 m Real[L] (instantiates to Float[L] where one is expected) 3 (coherent SI)
3.0 m Float[L] (may be exact where Real[L] is expected) 3.0
25 °C Point[Θ] 298.15 K (§5.6)
1.5 kg*m^2/s^3 Float[M*L^2/T^3] 1.5
50 % Real[1] (Float[1] where a Float is expected) 0.5

The display unit (m, km, °C) is not part of the type. It is metadata on the value, and to(unit, x) changes it (§5.7).

1.9 Operators and punctuation

Precedence and associativity are in §2.6.

Token Meaning
+ - * / @ ^ arithmetic, matrix product, power
== != < <= > >= comparison
&& || ! logical and, or, not; postfix ! is the factorial (§2.7.5)
|> <| pipe, reverse application
:: list cons
.. ..= ... half-open and closed ranges (index position only), record row variable
-> => <- = : function type / match arm, lambda, do bind, definition, annotation
? √ postfix error propagation, prefix square root

Synonyms are normalised to ASCII tokens in the lexer, keeping the source position for diagnostics and formatting: · × = *, ÷ = /, − = -, ≤ ≥ ≠ = <= >= !=. % appears only inside unit literals.

1.10 Lexical errors

Code Situation
E0101 tab in indentation
E0102 inconsistent indentation (no matching level for a DEDENT)
E0103 indented block required but absent
E0104 unclosed string, block comment or bracket; empty {} interpolation
E0105 invalid character or unknown escape
E0106 invalid numeric literal (underscore placement, no digits)
E0107 invalid superscript sequence
E0108 missing unit after a numeric literal

A lexical error MUST report an exact line and column range and SHOULD suggest a fix.

02 — Syntax

This chapter defines the syntax of declarations, expressions, patterns and types, and explains how the ambiguous places are parsed. The full EBNF is in appendix A and is not repeated here.

2.1 Structure of a source file

module Kinematics            # optional; must be the first declaration

import Units.SI as SI        # qualified access
import Math { sqrt, pi }     # selective open
open Units.SI                # open units and constants

dimension Currency           # a user base dimension
unit EUR : Currency = 1      # a unit declaration

type Vec2 = { x: Real[L], y: Real[L] }

fn fall_time(h: Float[L]): Float[T] = sqrt(2 * h / g)

A file is one module. The order of top-level declarations does not affect name resolution (unit names are resolved after all declarations are parsed). A top level may contain module (at most once, and first), import/open, dimension/unit, type/class/instance/derive, fn/let and test. private before type, dimension, unit, fn, let or class keeps the declaration out of the export list; everything else is exported. The operand of open is a module path, not an alias introduced by import … as (§7.2).

2.2 Declarations

2.2.1 Functions

fn kinetic_energy(m: Real[M], v: Real[L/T]): Real[M*L^2/T^2] =
    m * v^2 / 2

fn norm((x, y): (Real[L], Real[L])): Real[L] = sqrt(x^2 + y^2)
  • Parameters may carry type annotations; when omitted they are inferred from the body and the call sites. A parameter may be a pattern.
  • The return type goes after ) and before =. The body is one expression; across several lines it is a block (§1.4.4).
  • A function declaration is recursive: fn f(x) = e is sugar for let rec f = fn (x) => e.

2.2.2 Values

let g = 9.80665 m/s^2
let (x, y) = (1.0 m, 2.0 m)
let rec fact = fn (n: Int) => if n == 0 then 1 else n * fact(n - 1)
  • A plain let is not recursive: in let x = x + 1 the right-hand x refers to an outer binding, and is E0301 if there is none. let rec makes the name visible in its own definition.
  • Top-level and local let behave the same. The pattern MUST match the type of the right-hand side (E0308); a constructor pattern binding (let Some(x) = e) is treated as irrefutable.

2.2.3 Types

type Vec2 = { x: Real[L], y: Real[L] }

type Shape =
    | Circle(Real[L])
    | Rect(Real[L], Real[L])

type Result<a, e> = | Ok(a) | Err(e)
  • type Name = T is a transparent alias.
  • type Name (no =) is an abstract type; its constructors and operations come from the standard library or a plugin.
  • type Name = | C1(…) | C2(…) is a nominal variant type; constructors are values/functions. A one-line variant list MUST start with | to distinguish it from an alias (type T = A(Int) | B is E0202); the indented form may omit the first |.
  • A recursive occurrence must be to the right of a function arrow or inside a record field, tuple or constructor argument (positivity, §3.6.4).
  • Constructor names are visible module-wide; qualify with Module.Ctor when two modules use the same name.

2.2.4 Classes and instances

class Show a where
    show(x: a): String

instance Show for Real[d] where
    show(x) = format_quantity(x)

derive Eq, Show for Vec2
  • A class has at least one type parameter. Methods have signatures only; default implementations come from derive or a plugin.
  • An instance head is instance Class for Type; the d in Real[d] is implicitly universally quantified. Coherence and the orphan rule are in §7.4.
  • derive generates the usual instances for records and variants.

2.2.5 Units and dimensions

dimension Currency
unit USD : Currency = 1
unit EUR : Currency = 1.08 USD
unit m : L = 1 prefixable
unit degC : Θ = 1 K offset 273.15 K

Syntax and semantics are in chapter 05.

2.2.6 Tests

test "fall time from 20 m" =
    approx_eq(fall_time(20.0 m), 2.019 s, 0.001 s)

test "energy is positive" =
    forall(fn (v: Real[L/T]) => kinetic_energy(1 kg, v) >= 0 J)

The name is a string and the body is a Bool (or Prop, §8.13) expression; aevia test collects and runs them. forall is a standard-library function, not a keyword, and its predicate takes one argument.

2.2.7 Attributes

@stable
@deprecated("use new_api")
fn old_api(x: Real[1]): Real[1] = ...

@op("mydomain/softplus")
fn softplus(x: Real[1]): Real[1] = ln(1 + exp(x))
  • Known attributes: @stable, @experimental, @deprecated (stability, §13.8); @jit (request JIT; E1001 if the backend lacks core.jit); @op and @rule (register an RSSN operator and a rewrite rule, §13.5); @bench (§9.12).
  • An unknown attribute is E0210. Attribute arguments MUST be compile-time constants.

2.3 Expressions

2.3.1 Blocks

A block is a sequence of let/fn declarations followed by one final expression (§1.4.4). A let is in scope to the end of the block.

let y =
    let a = 1
    let b = 2
    a + b

2.3.2 let … in

let x = let y = 2 in y^2 + 1

let … in is the non-recursive one-line form; for recursion use let rec or fn in a block.

2.3.3 if

let s = if x > 0 then "positive" else "non-positive"

if is an expression and MUST have an else; else if chains. The two branches MUST have the same type, dimension included. In a multi-line if, else is in the column of if (§1.4.4).

2.3.4 match

fn area(s: Shape): Real[L^2] =
    match s with
    | Circle(r) -> pi * r^2
    | Rect(w, h) -> w * h

fn sign(x: Real[1]): Int =
    match x with
    | 0 -> 0
    | n if n > 0 -> 1
    | _ -> -1
  • Arms are tried in order. A guard if is evaluated after the pattern matches and MUST be Bool. An arm body may be a block.
  • A non-exhaustive match is E0309; a redundant arm is warning W0201. Exhaustiveness analysis is in §2.4.
  • Three layouts: arms indented below match; arms in the same column as match; or inline after with. Brackets contain no NEWLINE, so println(match o with | Some(v) -> v | None -> 0) can only be written inline. An inline arm body ends at the next | or where the enclosing expression ends; a nested inline match swallows the following arms, so parenthesise it. The formatter prints the inline form inside expressions and the block form at line level.

2.3.5 Lambdas

let square = fn (x: Real[L]) => x^2
let add = fn (x, y) => x + y
let inc = fn x => x + 1
let f = fn (x: Real[L]) =>
    let y = x^2
    y + 1

Parameter and return types may be omitted. Closures capture lexically. fn (x, y) => … takes two arguments; there is no automatic currying (curry/uncurry, §8.3).

2.3.6 Calls and placeholders

f(x, y)
g()(x)                 # call the returned function
map(_, xs)             # fn f => map(f, xs)
map(f, _)              # fn ys => map(f, ys)

A call needs parentheses; arguments are comma-separated; there are no variadic functions; calls associate to the left. _ as an argument means "an argument is missing here": the whole call becomes a lambda whose parameters follow the order of the _s. _ anywhere else is E0202.

2.3.7 Operators and pipes

let y = x |> square |> sqrt          # sqrt(square(x))
let z = sqrt <| square <| x          # same
let w = xs |> map(f, _) |> sum
  • a |> f and f <| a both mean f(a). The right-hand side is a function value with no implicit currying, so a multi-argument function uses a placeholder (xs |> map(f, _)). The two operators share one level and associate to the left in order of appearance; use one direction per expression.
  • An operator section such as (== 1) or (* 2) is E0205; write fn y => y == 1.

Status: a bare operator (+) parses but its name is not yet bound to a function (E0301); write a lambda for now.

2.3.8 Records

let p = { x = 1.0 m, y = 2.0 m }
let q = { p with x = 3.0 m }
let r = { x, y }                      # field punning
let a = p.x
  • Records are structural; field order does not affect type equality. A duplicate field is E0208. {} is the empty record.
  • { p with x = … } builds a new record: unlisted fields are unchanged, and new fields may be added.
  • Field access is the highest-precedence postfix operator.

2.3.9 Variant construction

let s = Circle(1.0 m)
let n = None

A constructor is an ordinary function or value: Circle : Real[L] -> Shape, and None is a polymorphic value of Option<a>. Constructor names MUST start with an upper-case letter.

2.3.10 Tensors

let v = [1.0, 2.0, 3.0]              # Tensor<[3], Float>
let m = [[1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]]     # Tensor<[2,2], Float>
let e: Tensor<1, Float> = []         # an empty tensor needs an annotation
let q = zeros<Int>(3, 4)             # explicit element type; default Float
  • Nested literals build rank-n tensors and MUST be rectangular, otherwise E0203. The element type is unified across elements ([1, 2.0] is Float); an unannotated [] is E0314.
  • Explicit type arguments are written f<T>(…) with < touching the function name; a comparison < has a space before it (§2.7.2).

2.3.11 Indexing and slicing

let x = v[0]                         # element
let row = m[1]                       # rank 2 → rank 1
let a = m[0, 1]                      # element
let col = m[.., 1]                   # column
let block = m[0..2, 1..3]            # sub-matrix
let last = v[-1]                     # negative index: from the end
let ev = v[::2]                      # strided slice
  • Indices start at 0 and negative ones count from the end. .. is half-open and ..= closed; an omitted end means the boundary. a[i] takes an element at rank 1 and the i-th slice (rank minus one) at rank > 1; a[i, j] needs rank ≥ 2.
  • a[i:j:s] is a strided slice; i, j and s may each be omitted (s defaults to 1), and a[i:j] is the same node as a[i..j].
  • :: is always cons (§2.6), so xs[1::2] is xs[1 :: 2]. With a start and a stride, write the end (xs[1:5:2]); when the end is omitted the start must be omitted too (xs[::2]). Inside [...] a : is the slice separator, so an annotated subscript needs parentheses: xs[(i : Int)].
  • An out-of-bounds index is run-time E0802; the compiler checks literal indices statically. A slice is semantically a copy, and the runtime may share the buffer; a negative-stride slice is always a view (§6.6.4.1).

2.3.12 do and IO

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    let name = "data.csv"
    text <- read_file(name)
    let rows = parse_csv(text)
    println("rows = {length(rows)}")
    pure(())
  • x <- action binds the result of an IO action; let is a pure binding; a bare IO expression runs in order and its result is discarded (warning W0202 when the type is not IO Unit); a statement that is not IO is E0701.
  • The value of do is its last expr statement. A do statement cannot declare fn or let rec; write a block expression when you need one.
  • pure : a -> IO a is a Prelude function, not a keyword (§9.7.3). do accepts only IO.

2.3.13 Error propagation ?

e? requires e : Result<T, E> in a function returning Result<U, E>: on success it yields T, on failure it returns Err(e) early. ? also works on Option in a function returning Option.

2.3.14 Other expressions

()                                   # unit
(1, 2)                               # tuple
(1)                                  # parentheses, not a tuple
x : Real[L]                          # type annotation
x!                                   # factorial (Int)
√x                                   # sqrt(x)
  • There are no one-element tuples ((1,) is E0202); use the value or a record.
  • A type annotation e : T has the lowest precedence and covers the whole expression.
  • The rules for x! are in §2.7.5. √x is the prefix form of sqrt(x); the operand's type MUST already be known at that point (√x works for an annotated x; √4.0 cannot yet be inferred, so write sqrt(4.0)).

2.4 Patterns

let (x, y) = p
let { x, y } = point
let [a, b, ..rest] = xs

match shape with
| Circle(r) -> …
| Rect(w, h) if w > h -> …
| _ -> …

The grammar is in A.4.

  • A variable pattern binds a name; _ binds nothing; p as x binds the whole value while matching p; (p : T) annotates.
  • An upper-case name in a binding position is a variable. In let, a let inside do, <-, and function and lambda parameters, a bare UIDENT (let A = …, fn step(M: Tensor<2, Float>)) binds a variable, because scientific code habitually names matrices and constants in capitals. A constructor pattern in a binding position MUST carry an argument list (let Some(x) = …); a nullary constructor is matched as a constructor only in match arms. The bound variable shadows a value of the same name, units included, but does not affect unit literals such as 3 A.
  • A constructor pattern uses the nominal type and constructor index fixed by the checker; equal display names do not make equal constructors.
  • A record pattern matches only the listed fields (open matching); { x, y } binds the same-named fields and { x = p } renames. A tuple pattern needs equal arity.
  • Tensor patterns support rank 1 only: [a, b, ..rest]. Without ..rest the length must be exactly equal; with it the length must be at least the prefix length, and rest keeps the element representation, units and nominal schema and shares the original storage.
  • A binding name MUST be unique within one pattern (shorthand, as and rest included), otherwise E0308.
  • A floating literal is not a valid pattern (E0204); use a guard.
  • Exhaustiveness keeps correlations between fields: (true,true) and (false,false) do not cover (true,false); [] and [head, ..tail] cover all lengths; guards do not contribute to coverage. The analysis has a fixed work and depth budget; when it cannot prove exhaustiveness it asks for a _ arm and never assumes coverage.

2.5 Type syntax

Real[L/T]
Float[1]
Tensor<2, Float[L]>
Tensor<[3, ?], Real[d]>
{ x: Real[L], ...r }
(a, b) -> c
forall d. Real[d] -> Real[d^2]
Point[Θ]
Result<T, E>
IO Unit

The grammar is in A.5. In brief:

  • Real, Float and Complex without brackets mean [1].
  • In Tensor<2, Float> the 2 is the rank; Tensor<[3,4], Float> is a refined shape, and ? marks an unknown extent.
  • Unit[d] is the type of unit values (§5.2); bare Unit is the unit type ().
  • A tuple type is (A, B). A * B is not a type; * appears only in dimensions.
  • The kind of a type_param (Type, Dim, Nat, Row, Rep, Format, Shape) may be omitted and is inferred from use. <…> on a declaration already quantifies universally, so forall is usually omitted.
  • IO applies to one atomic type: IO Unit, IO (Result<T, E>).

2.5.1 Dimension syntax

  • A dim is factors joined by * and /; a factor may carry an exponent ^n or ^(p/q), and 1 is dimensionless. / associates to the left: L/T^2 = L/(T^2).
  • A capitalised name is a base dimension or alias; a lower-case name is a dimension variable and may appear only inside […] (a bare d is a type variable).
  • Dimension aliases (Velocity, Force) are expanded during checking and preferred when printing.

2.6 Operator precedence and associativity

From lowest to highest:

Level Operators Associativity
1 |> <| left (in order of appearance when mixed)
2 || right
3 && right
4 == != < <= > >= non-associative (chaining is E0202)
5 :: right
6 + - left
7 * / @ · × ÷ left
8 prefix - + ! √ prefix
9 ^ right
10 call, index, .field, ?, postfix ! postfix

.. and ..= occur only in indices. -x^2 = -(x^2); 2^3^2 = 2^(3^2); a * b @ c = (a * b) @ c, so parenthesise matrix products; write x == y && y == z for x == y == z. The bodies of if, match, fn and let extend as far right as possible; parenthesise to break ambiguity. | is used only for match arms and variant declarations.

2.7 Ambiguities and their resolution

2.7.1 An identifier after a number

3 m is a unit literal, and so is 3 x (E0501 when x is not a unit); see §1.8.1.

2.7.2 < for type arguments

In zeros<Float>(3, 4) the < touches zeros and is parsed as type arguments; in a < b the < is preceded by a space and is a comparison. After ident< the parser tries type arguments and falls back to a comparison on failure, so the formatter MUST keep the space before a comparison <. An adjacent <…> is valid only when the callee is a function name (or its qualified name); 3<Int>(2) and f()<Int> are E0207, which suggests adding a space or parenthesising the callee.

2.7.3 { is a record, not a block

Blocks use indentation only, and {…} is always a record: if x then { y = 1 } else { y = 2 } is a record expression.

2.7.4 The roles of |

| appears in match arms (| pattern -> expr) and variant declarations (| Ctor(T)). Logical or is the two-character ||. There are no anonymous union types.

2.7.5 Prefix and postfix !

  • Prefix !x is logical not; the != of x != y is a single token.
  • Postfix x! is the factorial and x!! the double factorial. The ! MUST touch the end of its operand (no whitespace) and the operand MUST be an Int; n ! is a syntax error. Postfix ! binds tighter than prefix - and than ^: -3! = -6 and 2^3! = 2^6. For the factorial of a compound expression write factorial(n + 1).

Status: (n + 1)!, with ! right after a closing parenthesis, is currently rejected by the parser (E0202); write factorial(n + 1).

2.7.6 Unary -

Unary - binds more loosely than ^ and more tightly than *: -2^2 = -4 and (-2)^2 = 4. a - -b is valid.

2.8 Desugaring

These forms serve type checking and the core IR (§9.2.2); they are not user syntax.

Syntax Desugars to
fn f(x) = e let rec f = fn (x) => e
a |> f, f <| a f(a)
√x sqrt(x)
x!, x!! factorial(x), double_factorial(x)
a @ b matmul_rank(a, b) (dispatches on rank to matmul/matvec/vecmat/dot)
a * b (tensors) mul_elementwise(a, b)
{ p with x = e } the record-update primitive
f(_, x) fn a => f(a, x)
do … a chain of bind/pure
[a, b, c] tensor([a, b, c])
a[i, j], a[i..j], a[i:j:s] index(a, [i, j]), slice(a, i, j), slice_step(a, i, j, s)

2.9 A complete example

module Projectile

open Units.SI
import Math { sqrt, atan, sin, cos }

type State = {
    x:  Float[L],
    y:  Float[L],
    vx: Float[L/T],
    vy: Float[L/T],
}

type Rate = {
    x:  Float[L/T],
    y:  Float[L/T],
    vx: Float[L/T^2],
    vy: Float[L/T^2],
}

let g = 9.80665 m/s^2

fn rhs(_t: Float[T], s: State): Rate =
    let speed = sqrt(s.vx^2 + s.vy^2)
    let k = 0.02 / m
    {
        x  = s.vx,
        y  = s.vy,
        vx = -k * speed * s.vx,
        vy = -g - k * speed * s.vy,
    }

fn trajectory(v0: Float[L/T], angle: Float[1]): Tensor<1, State> =
    let s0 = { x = 0.0 m, y = 0.0 m,
               vx = v0 * cos(angle), vy = v0 * sin(angle) }
    let ts = linspace(0.0 s, 10.0 s, 1000)
    odeint(rhs, s0, ts)

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    let path = trajectory(50.0 m/s, 45 deg)
    println("range = {path[-1].x}")

The example uses records, field access, units, dimension inference, mixing Float and Real, tensors, ODE integration, do and string interpolation (chapter 11 walks through it line by line). Chapter 03 explains where the types of these expressions come from.

03 — Type system

Aevia's type system is HM inference + dimension algebra + type classes + row polymorphism. It has no full dependent types and no subtyping; flexibility comes from parametric, dimension and row polymorphism. This chapter defines the structure of types, the inference rules and the defaulting policy. Dimension algebra is chapter 04.

3.1 Kinds

Kind Meaning Example
Type value types Int, Real[L], Tensor<[3,4], Float>
Dim dimension expressions L, L/T, d
Nat type-level naturals 0, 2, n
Row record rows the r in { x: Real[L], ...r }
Rep numeric representation; closed: Int, Real, Float, Complex ρ
Format sparse format; closed: Csr, Csc, Coo, Block, Bcsr Csr
Shape type-level shapes [3,4], [n,m], [?]

Users cannot extend Rep or Format.

3.2 Types

3.2.1 Scalars Scalar<ρ, δ>

All numeric scalars share one form, Scalar : Rep -> Dim -> Type, with four surface aliases:

Written Internal form Meaning
Int Scalar<Int, 1> arbitrary-precision integer, dimensionless
Real[d] Scalar<Real, d> exact real or formal symbolic expression
Float[d] Scalar<Float, d> IEEE-754 binary64, never symbolic
Complex[d] Scalar<Complex, d> complex number (exact/symbolic or Complex64)

Real, Float and Complex without brackets mean [1]. An Int is always dimensionless: an exact integer with a dimension is a Real[L] whose value happens to be an integer, so Int is safe for indexing and counting. A Float is strictly an f64; expressions containing symbols belong to Real, so Real + Float is Real (the Float is converted exactly to a rational and symbolic information survives: pi + 1.0 stays pi + 1). To stay on the floating path write to_float(x) or make every operand a Float.

3.2.2 Other type constructors

()            : Type                         the unit value
Bool          : Type                         concrete boolean
Prop          : Type                         symbolic proposition (§3.8.6)
String        : Type
Tensor        : Shape -> Type -> Type        tensor (rank or refined shape)
Matrix        : Nat -> Nat -> Type -> Type   = Tensor<[n,m], τ>
Sparse        : Format -> Shape -> Type -> Type   sparse tensor (§13.9.5)
Point         : Dim -> Type                  affine point (Float representation; may be a tensor element)
Unit          : Dim -> Type                  unit value
Table         : Type -> Type                 row-polymorphic table; argument is a closed record type (§13.9.3)
Dynamic       : Type                         unknown schema (only Table<Dynamic>)
Device        : Type                         run-time device (§13.9.4)
Set Option    : Type -> Type
Result        : Type -> Type -> Type
IO            : Type -> Type
(->)          : multi-argument function type (τ1, …, τn) -> τ; () -> τ takes no arguments
SymFun        : Dim -> Dim -> Type           symbolic function (for dsolve)
Dyn           : Rep -> Type                  dynamically dimensioned scalar (§4.9)
RateOf        : Type -> Type                 time-derivative type of an ODE state (§8.9)
DiffusionOf   : Type -> Type                 diffusion-coefficient type of an SDE state (§8.9.1)
Solutions     : Rep -> Dim -> Type           symbolic solution set (§8.6)
  • Tensor<0, τ> is by definition τ, so the typing rule for indexing is uniformly "rank minus one".
  • Point[d] has only a Float representation; a symbolic temperature uses a Real[Θ] difference (§5.6).
  • Unit[d] is the type of unit values (m : Unit[L]), not ().
  • DiffusionOf<s> is isomorphic to RateOf<s> except that every leaf's dimension is further divided by T^(1/2) (the Brownian increment dW has dimension T^(1/2)). A state with Int, Bool or function fields is E0302.
  • The Prelude and libraries also define Ordering (Less | Equal | Greater) and the opaque or error types DimValue, DimError, EvalError, MathError, DataError, LinAlgError, Record, Json, Value, OpError, Plugin, PluginId, Callback, TypeRep and TermRef (§4.9, §13.3, §13.5).

3.2.3 Records and rows

{ x: Real[L], y: Real[L] }              closed record
{ x: Real[L], ...r }                    open record, r : Row

A record type is { l1: τ1, …, ln: τn | ρ } where ρ is a row variable or the empty row; field order is irrelevant.

3.2.4 Variants

Variants are nominal: type Shape = | Circle(Real[L]) | Rect(Real[L], Real[L]) is not equal to any structurally identical record or variant. Constructors may take type parameters: type Option<a> = | None | Some(a).

3.2.5 Type schemes

σ ::= ∀a:κ. σ | C => σ | τ. ∀ quantifies type, dimension, natural, row and representation variables. C includes class constraints (Show a) and numeric constraints: the representation domain of a literal, Join(ρ1, ρ2, ρout), Divide(ρin, ρout) and the input-dependent private-literal reading rules. Constraints are stored with schemes, function aliases and module interfaces and are rebuilt by the compiler; users never write them. Instantiation renames the variables in constraints too. Intermediate representations produced by operations are instantiated independently but do not become explicitly selectable <…> parameters.

3.3 Environments and inference

3.3.1 Environment

Γ ::= ∅ | Γ, x : σ | Γ, α:κ | Γ, C. Top-level declarations are checked by first collecting signatures and then checking bodies, so declarations in a module may refer to one another (recursion included).

3.3.2 Bidirectional checking

  • Synthesis Γ ⊢ e ⇒ τ infers a type from an expression; checking Γ ⊢ e ⇐ τ checks against a known expected type.
  • A literal synthesises a fresh type with a literal constraint; a variable instantiates its scheme; a call synthesises the function type and unifies it with the arguments; a lambda checks its parameters against an expected type, or gives them fresh type variables; let infers the right-hand side and then generalises, let rec first binds a fresh type; if requires a Bool condition and unifies the branches; match checks patterns against the scrutinee type and unifies the arm bodies; record and tensor literals unify field by field and element by element; an annotation (e : τ) uses checking mode.

3.3.3 Generalisation and weak variables

At let and top-level declarations, type, dimension and row variables that do not occur free in Γ are universally quantified together with their class constraints:

let id = fn (x) => x
# id : ∀a. a -> a
let mean = fn (xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>) => sum(xs) / length(xs)
# mean : ∀d. Tensor<1, Real[d]> -> Real[d]

Weak variables are not generalised. They serve values whose name must have one dimension at every use:

let x = symbol("x")        # x : Real[d], d is a weak variable
let a = x + 3 m            # d unifies with L
let b = x + 2 s            # E0401: x is already L

A weak variable still unresolved at the end of its declaration defaults to 1 with warning W0301. Generalising to ∀d. Real[d] would let one symbol be taken for different dimensions at different places, so it is not done.

  • symbol : String -> Real[d] and symfun : String -> SymFun<d,e> are built-in primitives. Each syntactic call allocates fresh weak variables; when a call runs repeatedly in a loop or function the runtime creates new symbols but the type remains the same weak variable.
  • let f = symbol passes symbol as a value: every call of f then shares one dimension (a symbol factory has one dimension). Weak variables carry a level; generalisation excludes weak variables and those reachable from their substitutions, so they cannot escape polymorphically through a closure: in let mk = fn s => symbol(s) the d stays weak.
  • An empty tensor [] introduces no weak variable and needs an expected type (E0314).
  • Aevia's Symbol is Real[δ] with δ weak; there is no fifth kind of value. When the Rssn façade is generated, a Symbol parameter in a signature reads as a fresh weak Real[δ] and a Real[d] parameter as Dim(Real, d). So the signature (Term, Symbol) -> Term of diff reads (Dyn<term>, Real[δ]) -> Dyn<term>, with the weak variable unified at the call site.

3.3.4 The value restriction

A pure language has no mutable references and needs no value restriction: any expression may be generalised. Weak variables are not a value restriction; they enforce the identity of a name.

3.4 The numeric tower and promotion

3.4.1 Promotion lattice

Int < Float < Complex        Int < Real < Complex

Float and Real are incomparable, and join picks Real when they meet (exact wins). An Int promotes to any other representation; a Float promotes exactly to Real (every f64 is a rational, though numerator and denominator may be large) or to Complex; a Real promotes to Complex; a Complex never demotes. Real → Float happens only through an explicit to_float and fails if free symbols remain. Promotion happens only when an operation needs it.

join(Int, Int) = Int       join(Int, Real) = Real       join(Int, Float) = Float
join(Real, Real) = Real    join(Real, Float) = Real     join(Float, Float) = Float
join(_, Complex) = Complex

An unresolved representation keeps a finite arithmetic relation instead of being identified with one input: Join(ρ1, ρ2, ρout) constrains the three by the table above and Divide(ρin, ρout) constrains the promotion of integer division. An ordinary lattice bound cannot replace them.

fn half<r: Rep>(x: r) = x / 2
let exact = half<Int>(3)        # Real, exactly 3/2
let floating = half<Float>(3.0) # Float

half has the single explicit parameter r; a caller cannot pick a result representation that contradicts the division rule. The relations are kept both at check time and in the run-time generic proof of a closure.

3.4.2 Arithmetic typing rules

Let ρ = join(ρ1, ρ2) and ρ' = div_rep(ρ) (div_rep(Int) = Real, otherwise unchanged):

Expression Operands Result
a + b, a - b, min(a,b) Scalar<ρ1,d>, Scalar<ρ2,d> Scalar<ρ,d>
a * b Scalar<ρ1,d>, Scalar<ρ2,e> Scalar<ρ,d*e>
a / b same Scalar<ρ',d/e>
a ^ n Scalar<ρ,d>, integer literal Scalar<ρ',d^n>
a ^ q Scalar<ρ,d>, rational literal Scalar<ρ,d^q>
-a, abs(a) Scalar<ρ,d> Scalar<ρ,d>
sign(a) Scalar<ρ,d> Int
clamp(x,lo,hi) all of dimension d Scalar<ρ,d>

Division, negative exponents, roots and inversion promote integers to exact rationals: Int / Int is Real[1] (7 / 2 is exactly 7/2 and cannot be annotated Int); integer division is div(a,b) or quot(a,b); 2^-1 is the Real 1/2. min, max, mod and rem require ρ ≠ Complex; the modulus of a complex number is magnitude (§8.15), not abs.

3.4.3 Static limits on powers

When the exponent is a compile-time integer or rational, the exponent is computed in the type: (3 m)^2 : Real[L^2], sqrt(4 m^2) : Real[L], (2 m)^(1/2) : Real[L^(1/2)]. When the exponent is a run-time Int:

  • A dimensionless base (Scalar<ρ,1>) is allowed; the result is dimensionless.
  • A base whose dimension δ is still an unsolved inference variable (such as t in diff(fn t => t^t)): a run-time exponent requires a dimensionless base, which is a constraint and not an error, so the checker sets δ = 1 — the same rule by which sin(t) makes t dimensionless.
  • A base whose dimension is known and is not 1: E0404. Use pow_dyn(a, n) : Dyn<ρ> (§4.9), or write exp(n * ln(a / unit)). In a generic fn f<d>(x: Real[d], n: Int), x^n is rejected because d is not constrained to 1.

This is the edge of a static dimension system: the exponent of d^n is a run-time value the type level cannot represent, and Dyn is the explicit exit.

3.4.4 Transcendental functions

Function Argument Result
sin cos tan asin acos atan, sinh … atanh, exp ln log log2 log10 Scalar<ρ,1> Scalar<ρ,1>
atan2(y,x) same d Scalar<ρ,1>
hypot(x,y) same d Scalar<ρ,d>
sqrt(x) / cbrt(x) Scalar<ρ,d>, ρ ∈ {Real,Float} d^(1/2) / d^(1/3)
floor ceil round trunc, mod(a,b) Scalar<ρ,d> (mod: both d) Scalar<ρ,d>

sin(3 m) is E0403; sin(30 deg) is valid because deg is dimensionless (§5.5).

3.5 Tensor types

3.5.1 Rank and shape

Tensor<n, τ>                 # rank only; the shape is a run-time value
Tensor<[n1, …, nk], τ>       # refined shape
Matrix<n, m, τ>              # = Tensor<[n, m], τ>
  • Tensor<n, τ> is sugar for Tensor<[?, …, ?], τ> (n question marks). A rank error is a compile-time error; a shape error is a compile-time error under a refined type and a run-time E0802 otherwise. A rank n MUST be a concrete integer (E0304).
  • The extents of a refined shape are type-level Nats (literals, variables, +, multiplication by a literal, or ?). Literals acquire a refined shape automatically, and constructors such as zeros_of<[3,4]>() can state one explicitly. The shape argument of Tensor is a special type form, not an ordinary type parameter.
  • Refined → rank-only is a safe implicit conversion. The reverse uses refine_shape(a) : Option<Tensor<[…],τ>>, a run-time check; annotating directly is E0302. To branch on shape use refine_shape<[3, ?]>(a) (§8.4).
  • reshape<[n1, …, nk]>(a) always returns Result<Tensor<shape,τ>, ShapeError>: when the shape is statically known the element count is checked at compile time and the result is always Ok; otherwise it is checked at run time. reshape_dyn always checks at run time.
  • Indexing: a refined shape with a literal subscript is checked at compile time against -n_i <= i < n_i; a dynamic subscript is checked at run time.

Nat equations. Unification treats equations as polynomials:

  • With one variable the equation is solved over non-negative integers: n + 1 ~ 3 ⇒ n = 2; 2·n ~ 6 ⇒ n = 3; n ~ 2·n ⇒ n = 0. An equation in several variables is kept as a pending constraint and retried after later bindings; a non-zero constant residue is E0302.
  • A product of variables (n*m) is an opaque monomial and cancels only against the same product (n*m ~ m*n); once one factor is solved it is substituted back into a linear equation. A product that stays unsolved is reported at the end of the declaration as E0304, with the equation. A shape variable without a binder, and a Nat variable in an instance head, are also E0304.
  • Variables declared in fn f<n, m> are rigid: a literal or expression may solve them, but two distinct declared variables are never merged, and n ~ m is E0302. Variables created by instantiating a generic callee are inference variables and may cancel against any expression, so a generic function with symbolic extents may call another generic function.

Broadcasting. Element-wise operations act per axis: extents are equal, or one side is the literal 1 (the result takes the other side). [3,1] + [1,4] is [3,4] and [n,1] + [1,m] is [n,m]; two symbolic extents that are not 1 are unified; a rank-only side counts as unknown on every axis. Tensors of different rank do not broadcast (E0302, with a hint to build the larger shape explicitly with map or outer), so the result rank is always statically known and silent errors such as (3,1) + (1,4) cannot occur.

Shape refinement catches 3×4 versus 4×3, matrix-product dimensions and reshape element counts without full dependent types, while Tensor<n,τ> remains the erased form for "shape unknown / from a CSV". It has only linear Nat arithmetic, no general recursion and no proof terms.

3.5.2 Literals

[1, 2, 3]                 : Tensor<[3], Int>
[[1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]]  : Tensor<[2, 2], Float>
[1, 2.0]                  : Tensor<[2], Float>      # promotion
[]                        : needs an annotation

Nesting depth fixes the rank, sibling lengths MUST be equal (E0203), element types are unified, and an empty literal needs an expected type (E0314).

3.5.3 Element-wise operations

For Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ1,d>> and Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ2,e>>: + and - require d = e and give Scalar<join,d>; * and / give d*e and d/e; a ^ n (literal n) gives d^n; unary - and abs preserve the type. Mixing scalars or unit values with tensors:

Scalar<ρ1,d> * Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ2,e>> : Tensor<n, Scalar<join,d*e>>
Scalar<ρ1,d> + Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ2,e>> : Tensor<n, Scalar<join,d>>, requires d=e
Tensor<shape, Scalar<ρ,d>> * Unit[e]   : Tensor<shape, Scalar<ρ,d*e>>
Tensor<shape, Scalar<ρ,d>> / Unit[e]   : Tensor<shape, Scalar<ρ,d/e>>

A refined and a rank-only shape of the same rank unify in operations (refined → rank-only is an implicit conversion).

Element rules for Tensor<shape, Point[d]>: Point - Point : Float[d]; Point + Float[d] : Point; Point + Point is E0405. mean accepts numeric tensors only; average points with centroid : Tensor<1, Point[d]> -> Point[d].

3.5.3.1 Rank dispatch of @

@ dispatches on rank (same precedence as *, left-associative):

Left Right Result Function
Tensor<2, ·d> Tensor<2, ·e> Tensor<2, ·d*e> matmul
Tensor<2, ·d> Tensor<1, ·e> Tensor<1, ·d*e> matvec
Tensor<1, ·d> Tensor<2, ·e> Tensor<1, ·d*e> vecmat
Tensor<1, ·d> Tensor<1, ·e> Scalar<ρ,d*e> dot

Here ·d stands for Scalar<ρ,d>. Under refined shapes [m,k] @ [k,n] → [m,n], [m,k] @ [k] → [m] and [k] @ [k,n] → [n]; m, k and n are checked at compile time, and rank-only operands at run time. Sparse matrices use Sparse<F, shape, τ>; dense/sparse mixes dispatch on layout (§13.9.5) and never convert implicitly.

3.5.4 Indexing

In a : Tensor<n, τ> the rank n MUST be a known concrete natural. Each scalar subscript removes an axis and each slice (.., i..j, i:j:s) keeps one:

a[i]          : Tensor<n-1, τ>      requires n >= 1
a[i, j]       : Tensor<n-2, τ>      same as a[i][j]
a[i, ..]      : Tensor<n-1, τ>
a[.., j]      : Tensor<n-1, τ>
a[i..j, ..]   : Tensor<n, τ>
a[i..j, j]    : Tensor<n-1, τ>

Rank-polymorphic code uses map, fold and sum instead of subscripts. An out-of-bounds subscript is a run-time E0802; literal subscripts may be checked statically.

3.5.5 Dimension rules for reductions and linear algebra

Full signatures are in §8.4; only the dimension and representation rules are listed here:

Function Result
sum, min, max, norm, trace Scalar<ρ,d>
sum(a, axis) same d, rank kept (keepdims)
mean, std Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d>
var Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d^2>
normalize dimensionless elements
dot, matmul, matvec, outer d*e
det, prod accept dimensionless elements only
inv Result<Tensor<2, Scalar<div_rep(ρ),1/d>>, LinAlgError>
linsolve(A, b) Result<Tensor<1, Scalar<div_rep(ρ),e/d>>, LinAlgError>
eig eigenvalues of dimension d, dimensionless eigenvectors
svd, qr, lu singular values, R and U have dimension d; the other factors are dimensionless

The dimension of det is d^n with n a run-time shape, so det accepts only dimensionless matrices; a dimensioned determinant uses det_unit(a, u) : Dyn<ρ> (§4.9). prod is the same.

3.6 Records, rows and variants

3.6.1 Row unification

{ l: τ1 | ρ1 } ~ { l: τ2 | ρ2 }   ⇒  τ1 ~ τ2, ρ1 ~ ρ2
{ l: τ | ρ } ~ { l1: τ1, …, ln: τn | ρ' }
   ⇒ l matches some li, or l is absent and ρ absorbs the remaining fields

Row unification follows Rémy's algorithm and carries lacks constraints: when ρ lacks l, ρ may absorb a field only if it does not contain l, otherwise duplicate labels would arise. A missing field is E0306; a duplicate field is E0208. A record literal has a closed row, while a record annotation on a function parameter may be open:

fn norm(p: { x: Real[L], y: Real[L], ...r }): Real[L] =
    sqrt(p.x^2 + p.y^2)

norm({ x = 1 m, y = 2 m, z = 3 m }) is valid and binds r to { z: Real[L] }.

3.6.2 Record update

{ p with x = 3.0 m } requires p to have field x; the result has the type of p with x replaced by the new type. If the field does not exist it is added, which requires row(p) lacks z (otherwise E0208). Updates are pure and the old record is unchanged; the runtime updates in place when the record is uniquely owned.

3.6.3 Variants and exhaustiveness

A constructor is a function or a value: Circle : Real[L] -> Shape, None : Option<a>, Some : a -> Option<a>. A variant match MUST cover all constructors or end with _; record patterns are open; a redundant arm warns W0201. A constructor pattern with the wrong arity is E0307; a constructor call with the wrong number of arguments is E0315.

3.6.4 Positivity of recursive types

Recursive types are allowed (type Tree<a> = | Leaf | Node(Tree<a>, a, Tree<a>)), but a recursive occurrence may not be to the left of a function arrow: type Bad = | B(Bad -> Int) is E0312. Aevia proves nothing, but positivity keeps the construction of run-time values well-founded.

3.7 Functions, closures and tail calls

3.7.1 Function types

(τ1, …, τn) -> τ: arguments are passed by value, functions are closures and may be passed and returned. Function equality is undecidable, so == does not apply to functions (E0310: no Eq instance).

3.7.2 Multiple arguments

Functions take several arguments and are not curried automatically:

fn add(x: Int, y: Int): Int = x + y
add(1, 2)          # 3
add(1)             # E0315: wrong number of arguments
add(1, _)          # fn y => add(1, y)

Use curry/uncurry (§8.3) when currying is needed; the _ placeholder is lightweight partial application.

3.7.3 Tail calls

  • A self-recursive tail call MUST run in constant stack space; tail calls in mutual recursion are optimised as well.
  • Non-tail recursion is limited by the run-time stack; exceeding it is E0805 (stack overflow), never a silent crash.
fn sum_to(n: Int, acc: Int): Int =
    if n == 0 then acc else sum_to(n - 1, acc + n)

3.7.4 Recursion and effects

An IO action may be recursive, but only through a named function: a do block cannot declare fn or let rec (§2.3.12).

3.8 Type classes

3.8.1 Declarations and instances

class Show a where
    show(x: a): String

instance Show for Real[d] where
    show(x) = format_quantity(x)
  • A class has exactly one type parameter; class Convert a b is E0317.
  • Methods have signatures only; defaults come from derive, built-in structural implementations (Prelude classes) or plugins.
  • An instance head is a type-constructor application; a bare type-variable head instance C for a is E0317. Variables in the head are implicitly universally quantified.
  • An instance of a user class MUST define every method; the missing ones are listed in the E0317. Prelude classes are hooks: an instance may cover only some methods and the built-in structural implementation supplies the rest (such as printing records and tuples).
  • Class and instance identity is symbolic: a class key is (defining module path, class name) and an instance key adds the canonical head type. An AdtId is only an in-process witness and never enters an interface or cache. A private class is visible only in its defining module; a private instance is not allowed (instances are exported with the module, §7.4).

3.8.2 Resolution

A constraint C τ is resolved as follows: when τ is a concrete type constructor, the unique matching instance is looked up; when τ contains unresolved variables, resolution is deferred; if it is still unresolved at the end of the declaration, the error is E0310 (no instance) or E0304 (ambiguous). Instance matching is head matching without backtracking; overlapping instances are E0311.

3.8.3 Coherence and the orphan rule

One class and one type head have at most one instance. An instance MUST be defined in the module that owns the type or the module that owns the class (the orphan rule), so users cannot add Show to Int. This makes the set of instances deterministic under separate compilation.

3.8.4 Built-in classes

Class Methods Notes
Eq a == != concrete structural equality; no instance for functions or Real
Ord a compare, <, … partial order; compare : Option<Ordering> (None for NaN)
TotalOrd a total_compare total order required by Set, Map, sort; none for Float
Show a show readable form; used by string interpolation
Hash a hash hashed containers
Num a Fractional a Floating a arithmetic, from_int, sqrt, … dimensionless user numeric types only
Container f map, fold tensors, Option, Set
Sized a length String, Tensor
Arbitrary a arbitrary random generator for property tests
Parallel a par_map only Int/Float[d] and their tensors; symbolic Real is not parallelised
  • Num and friends do not apply to dimensioned scalars, because * changes the dimension and so does not satisfy a -> a -> a; dimensioned arithmetic is a built-in language rule.
  • Real is not Eq: x == y returns Prop (§3.8.6); use decide(x == y) or approx_eq for concrete equality. == on Real resolves to sym_eq, not through Eq, so generic code with an Eq a constraint is never instantiated at Real.
  • Float is Eq (NaN != NaN) but not TotalOrd, so Set<Float> is invalid; sort with sort_float (NaN last). Set<a> and Map<a,b> require TotalOrd a.
  • solve returns Solutions<Real,d> (§8.6), not a Set (symbolic roots have no total order).

Standard-library instance heads for Show, Eq, Ord and Hash MUST use the dimension- polymorphic form (Float[d]), otherwise 20.0 m could not be printed. Show for records, tuples, Tensor, Point, Solutions, Dyn and unit values is a structural guarantee: the built-in printer renders them when there is no user instance, and an instance a user writes for a nominal type they declared (or derive Show) takes priority. Functions have no Show instance and no structural fallback (E0310). The instance sets of built-in classes take part in global coherence (§7.4): two instances MUST be distinguishable by their run-time witness (§9.2.3), and equal witnesses are E0311 — for example Tensor<1, Int> and Tensor<1, Real> are both exact storage and cannot both have an instance of the same class.

3.8.5 Deriving

derive Eq, Show, Hash for Vec2 is open to structural classes only: Eq, Ord, TotalOrd, Show, Hash; deriving any other class is E0317. A derived instance is generated in the module that owns the type (the orphan rule applies) and Ord is lexicographic in field declaration order. Deriving fails for a function field, and the offending field type and class name appear in the E0317.

3.8.6 Propositions Prop

if, match and assert accept only Bool. Comparing Real values yields a proposition Prop:

(==) (!=) (<) (<=) (>) (>=) : Real[d] -> Real[d] -> Prop
decide   : Prop -> Option<Bool>
assuming : (Prop, () -> a) -> a
by_cases : (Prop, () -> Real[d], () -> Real[d]) -> Real[d]
  • Comparisons of Int, Float, String, records and variants return Bool; comparisons of Real return Prop, so no boolean is "guessed" at run time. Prop supports &&, || and !, and the result is still a Prop.
  • decide(p) returns Some(true/false) when it can prove or refute p, and None otherwise.
  • assuming(p, thunk) adds p as an assumption to the symbolic computation inside thunk; it is E0804 if p is decidably false. Only unary predicates are supported so far (x > 0, x >= 0, x != 0, x ∈ ℤ).
  • by_cases(p, yes, no) picks a branch when p is decidable and otherwise builds a symbolic ite term (symbolic derivation of piecewise functions).
  • A Prop in an if is E0302 (symbolic proposition is not a Bool); decide first, or rewrite with assuming/by_cases.

This distinction prevents a symbolic Bool from masquerading as a Bool: exhaustiveness of Bool, the Eq laws and the determinism of if all hold.

3.9 Effects: IO

3.9.1 Type

IO : Type -> Type and pure : a -> IO a; do desugars to a chain of bind (bind is not a function users can call). IO is a single effect type. Capabilities (fs, net, env, rand, gpu) are run-time permissions, not type parameters (§13.9.6), so standard-library signatures are not infested with effect rows while plugins and sandboxes can still declare the capabilities they need.

An IO a is an action description; effects occur only when the runtime executes main. A pure function cannot run IO.

3.9.2 Typing of do

Γ ⊢ e1 : IO a     Γ, x: a ⊢ rest : IO b
----------------------------------------
Γ ⊢ (x <- e1; rest) : IO b

Γ ⊢ e : IO a     a is not ()
--------------------------------
Γ ⊢ (e; rest) : IO b      (the result of e is discarded, warning W0202)

A let inside do is a pure binding.

3.9.3 Errors

Recoverable errors use Result<T, E> and ?; possible absence uses Option<T> (? works too); IO failures come back as a Result (for example read_file). There is no try/catch and no user-callable panic; an unrecoverable run-time error ends the program and prints the call stack (§6.9).

3.9.4 The purity boundary

IO in a pure context is E0313. An IO action may capture pure values but not vice versa. There is no unsafePerformIO. main : IO Unit is the only entry point; a library module may export IO actions for the caller to run.

3.10 Literals and defaults

3.10.1 Literal types

A literal is representation-polymorphic: it carries a Literal constraint, is instantiated where a type is expected, and is defaulted at the end of the declaration.

Literal Default type May instantiate to
123 Int Int, Real, Float, Complex
1.5, 1e3 Float[1] Float[1], Real[1] (exact decimal)
3 m Real[L] Real[L], Float[L], Complex[L]
3.0 m Float[L] Float[L], Real[L]
25 °C Point[Θ] Point[Θ] only (affine, fixed Float)
oo Real[1] Real[1], Float[1]; with a dimension write oo * 1 m
nan Float[1] Float[1]
  • An integer literal converts automatically where Real or Float is expected (2 in df(2) is a Real). A decimal literal converts to Real as an exact decimal: 1.5 is 3/2, so x + 1.5 (x : Real) stays exact.
  • The representation of a unit literal follows its coefficient: an integer coefficient defaults to Real and a decimal one to Float; an expected type may override (fall_time(20 m) instantiates 20 m as Float[L] where h : Float[L]).
  • 0 is not dimension-polymorphic and defaults to Int; zero length is 0 m and a floating zero is 0.0 (§4.7).

3.10.2 Defaulting

At the end of a declaration, variables still unresolved default in this order:

  1. a Rep variable from an integer literal → Int; 2. from a decimal literal → Float; 3. from a unit literal → Real (integer coefficient) or Float (decimal coefficient); 4. any other Rep variable → Real; 5. a Dim variable → 1, with warning W0301; 6. a Nat variable is not defaulted: if constraints cannot solve it, E0304; 7. a row variable → the empty row.

Defaulting touches only variables not quantified by a scheme or the lexical environment, and propagates all numeric constraints first. A Literal constraint chooses a legal default and never reads a decimal literal as Int. A private literal in a function body that depends on a generic input is, once the inputs are fixed, read first according to the arithmetic join of the inputs in the same numeric-constraint component, then by its literal default; unrelated parameters do not take part and the caller's expected result cannot choose this reading. A literal used directly as a generic parameter (fn zero<r: Rep>(): r = 0) keeps its own legal domain. Data that is actually built and captured keeps one type instance, and function aliases pass the construction requirement on, so a captured number is never read as both Int and Float.

3.10.3 Annotations take priority

With an expected type, a literal is checked against it and defaulting does not run:

let x: Float[L] = 0.0 m    # zero length, Float
let n: Int = 0
let y = 0                  # defaults to Int
let z: Real[L] = 0 m       # zero length, exact

A + - * / expression made only of literals and signs accepts the same local context: let exact: Real = 0.1 + 2 is exactly 21/10. The rule does not cross variables or calls: in fn f(x) = x + 1.0, f(1) is a Float and the caller cannot annotate it Int.

3.10.4 Literal folding

Folding is compile-time evaluation. It MUST give the same value as run-time evaluation; it is an optimisation, not a second arithmetic.

  1. A literal is rounded once. Decimal text is an exact rational; in a Float position it becomes the nearest f64. A unit literal multiplies its scale in exact arithmetic before rounding: 1.1 km : Float[L] is the f64 nearest 1100, not 1.1 * 1000.0 rounded twice; a scale with π (deg) multiplies the correctly rounded rational part by the f64 pi^k.
  2. A closed tree of literal arithmetic is a compile-time constant. An expression made only of literals, + - * / and signs is evaluated once at its static representation; a dimensionless tree of Float literals is folded to one constant at IR lowering with the same IEEE operations. So 0.1 + 0.2 : Float is 0.30000000000000004 whether written inline or via let.
  3. Exactness comes from types, not folding. 1.0 + 2 with no expected type is join(Int, Float) = Float, value 3.0; a decimal literal meeting an exact Real operand is read as an exact decimal: 1/3 + 0.1 is 13/30.

3.11 Dynamic dimensions Dyn (summary)

Static dimensions cannot express a run-time exponent x^n (n : Int) or a shape-dependent dimension (the d^n of det(A)). Dyn<ρ> is the explicit escape (representation kept):

dyn       : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Dyn<ρ>                       # d concrete
check     : Dyn<ρ> -> Result<Scalar<ρ,d>, DimError>     # d concrete
dyn_with  : (Scalar<ρ,d>, DimValue) -> Dyn<ρ>
check_with: (DimValue, Dyn<ρ>) -> Result<Scalar<ρ,1>, DimError>
dim_of    : Dyn<ρ> -> DimValue

dyn and check are type-directed primitives: d MUST be concrete at the call site and the compiler materialises it as a DimValue; generic code passes a run-time dimension explicitly with dyn_with/check_with. A Dyn never converts implicitly to a Scalar. The full rules are in §4.9.

3.12 Type errors

Code Situation
E0301 unbound name
E0302 type mismatch
E0303 occurs check (infinite type)
E0304 cannot infer / ambiguous
E0305 not a function
E0306 record lacks a field
E0307 constructor pattern arity mismatch
E0308 pattern does not match the scrutinee, or a pattern binds a name twice
E0309 non-exhaustive match
E0310 missing type-class instance
E0311 overlapping instances
E0312 non-positive recursive type
E0313 IO in a pure context
E0314 tensor element type or rank cannot be inferred
E0315 wrong number of arguments in a call
E0316 duplicate definition
E0317 class, instance or derive error

Dimension errors are E04xx (chapter 04) and unit errors E05xx (chapter 05).

3.13 Key inference rules

Γ(x) = σ    σ' = inst(σ)        Γ ⊢ e1 ⇒ τ1 -> τ2    Γ ⊢ e2 ⇐ τ1
-----------------------        --------------------------------
Γ ⊢ x ⇒ σ'                      Γ ⊢ e1(e2) ⇒ τ2

Γ, x: τ1 ⊢ e ⇐ τ2                Γ ⊢ e1 ⇐ Bool   Γ ⊢ e2 ⇐ τ   Γ ⊢ e3 ⇐ τ
--------------------------------  ------------------------------------------
Γ ⊢ (fn (x) => e) ⇐ τ1 -> τ2      Γ ⊢ if e1 then e2 else e3 ⇐ τ

Γ ⊢ e ⇒ { l: τ | ρ }             Γ ⊢ e1 ⇐ Scalar<ρ,d>   Γ ⊢ e2 ⇐ Scalar<ρ,d>
--------------------             ------------------------------------------
Γ ⊢ e.l ⇒ τ                      Γ ⊢ e1 + e2 ⇐ Scalar<ρ,d>

Γ ⊢ e1 ⇐ Scalar<ρ1,d>    Γ ⊢ e2 ⇐ Scalar<ρ2,e>
------------------------------------------------
Γ ⊢ e1 * e2 ⇐ Scalar<join(ρ1,ρ2), d*e>

The implementation order is: parse → name resolution → collect signatures → infer/check → default → export the interface. The dimension unification rules are in the next chapter.

04 — Dimensions
