This chapter is the heart of Aevia. Chapter 03 treated the d in Real[d] as a type parameter; here we define what d is, how dimensions are equal, solved and inferred, and what to do when the static system cannot express something.

4.1 Base dimensions

Canonical name Source / display alias Meaning
Length L length
Mass M mass
Time T time
Current I electric current
Temperature Θ thermodynamic temperature
Amount Amt amount of substance
Luminosity Lum luminous intensity
  • A base dimension is a generator, not a type constructor; 1 is dimensionless (the empty product). Names are case-sensitive and the full name is canonical: the boundaries of FFI, manifests and rssn::dim always use full names, while the single letters are only source and display aliases.
  • dimension Currency declares a new base dimension. Its identity is fixed by the canonical package/source owner of the defining module, the module path and the declared name; it is not a global bare name. Two packages that each declare Currency get different generators, while one declaration imported under different aliases is still equal. A prefix such as A.Currency is only a display or lookup hint and takes no part in equality, ordering, hashing or ADT schemas; an imported interface carries the resolved dimension. Instantiating a generic d: Dim replaces only dimension variables and keeps every nominal generator with its exponent.
  • Source base dimensions and host (rssn) dimensions are kept apart: a plain host Base("Currency") is never automatically equal to any source dimension Currency. A nominal generator crossing the bridge uses a versioned canonical encoding containing the full owner and declared name (ordinary host names under the reserved prefix are escaped to avoid collisions); the carrier is still RSSN's Base string, and the signature hash and binary layout of SI are unchanged. A generated façade module cannot redeclare an external owner by display name: such operators use a dynamic path, and units and constants of an external nominal dimension are marked "cannot be generated" and skipped, so the user imports them from their defining module.
  • A private dimension is visible only in its defining module. Public aliases, function signatures and type aliases may refer to it; the interface stores its resolved meaning, so users neither need nor can resolve the private name. An import is not a transitive export.

4.1.1 Derived aliases

Aliases are transparent and create no new type:

dimension Area          = L^2            dimension Volume       = L^3
dimension Velocity      = L/T            dimension Acceleration = L/T^2
dimension Frequency     = 1/T            dimension Force        = M*L/T^2
dimension Pressure      = M/(L*T^2)      dimension Energy       = M*L^2/T^2
dimension Power         = M*L^2/T^3      dimension Momentum     = M*L/T
dimension Density       = M/L^3          dimension Charge       = I*T
dimension Voltage       = M*L^2/(I*T^3)  dimension Resistance   = M*L^2/(I^2*T^3)
dimension Capacitance   = I^2*T^4/(M*L^2)  dimension Inductance = M*L^2/(I^2*T^2)
dimension MagneticFlux  = M*L^2/(I*T^2)  dimension MagneticFluxDensity = M/(I*T^2)
dimension Entropy       = M*L^2/(T^2*Θ)  dimension MolarMass    = M/Amt
dimension Conductance   = 1/Resistance   dimension Angle        = 1
dimension SolidAngle    = 1

Angle = 1 is deliberate: in SI the radian is dimensionless, sin accepts a dimensionless argument, and deg and rad are dimensionless units with scales (π/180, 1) (§5.5). An Angle base dimension would break exp(i*θ), sin(ω*t) and many other formulas. Aliases affect printing only: Real[M*L^2/T^2] and Real[Energy] are the same type, and printing prefers the shortest known alias.

4.2 Dimension expressions

δ ::= 1 | b | d | δ * δ | δ / δ | δ ^ q

b is a base dimension, d a dimension variable (kind Dim) and q a rational literal (2, -1, (1/2), (-3/4); the decimal 0.5 is invalid). The exponent of a static dimension is always a compile-time rational: when n is a run-time value the type level cannot represent d^n, and such cases use Dyn (§4.9). rssn's graph::dim supports a single symbolic exponent on the term graph; Aevia's static types do not.

4.2.1 Canonical form

A dimension is normalised to a finite map δ̂ : (Base ∪ DimVar) → Q keeping only generators with non-zero exponent: multiplication adds exponents, division subtracts, a power multiplies, zero entries are dropped, and generators are sorted in a fixed order (SI order, user bases, then variable names lexicographically). For example L * T^-1 ⇒ {L:1, T:-1}, L / L ⇒ {}, (L^2)^(1/2) ⇒ {L:1}. Two dimensions are equal if and only if their canonical forms are equal: L/T and L*T^-1, or L^2 and L^(4/2), are the same dimension.

Exponents are arbitrary-precision rationals, supporting at least i64 numerators and denominators; 1/2, 1/3, -1 and 2 MUST be exact. sqrt yields 1/2 and cbrt yields 1/3.

4.2.2 Dimension variables

A dimension variable d stands for "some unknown dimension" ranging over the whole free abelian group, and may appear in exponent positions: Real[d], Real[d^2], Real[L/d], Real[d^(1/2)].

fn square<d>(x: Real[d]): Real[d^2] = x^2
# square : ∀d. Real[d] -> Real[d^2]

4.3 Dimension unification

Dimension unification solves equations over an abelian group: given δ1 and δ2, find a substitution σ with σ(δ1) = σ(δ2).

4.3.1 Algorithm

Let q = normalize(δ1 / δ2), written as an exponent vector:

  1. If q = 1, succeed.
  2. Otherwise pick a dimension variable v with exponent r ≠ 0 (in name order, for reproducibility), set σ[v] = (q / v^r)^(-1/r), apply σ to q and return to step 1.
  3. If there is no variable and q ≠ 1, the dimensions mismatch (E0401).

Each step eliminates one variable, so the algorithm terminates. The solution never contains v itself, so an occurs-check failure cannot arise over an abelian group (E0409 is reserved and the present unifier never produces it). The most general unifier is not unique, but all choices are equivalent.

Examples

unify(L, T):      q = L/T, no variable, q ≠ 1 ⇒ mismatch
unify(d, L/T):    q = d·T/L, pick d: σ[d] = L/T
unify(d^2, d^3):  q = d^-1, pick d: σ[d] = 1  ⇒ d = 1
unify(d*e, L):    pick d: σ[d] = L/e, e stays free

The third example shows that a function without declared dimension parameters, fn f(x: Real[d], y: Real[d^3]) = x^2 + y, specialises d to 1; the same function with a declared <d> is rejected with E0318 (§4.4.1).

4.3.2 Diagnostics and integration

  • A dimension mismatch E0401 MUST list the canonical forms of both sides and the difference (for instance "the left has an extra T^-1").
  • Dimension unification runs when type unification meets Scalar<ρ1,d1> and Scalar<ρ2,d2>: unify(Scalar<ρ1,d1>, Scalar<ρ2,d2>) = unify(ρ1, ρ2) ∧ unify(d1, d2). Unifying ρ is ordinary first-order unification; dimension variables and type variables are stored separately and share substitution and levels.

4.4 Dimension polymorphism

4.4.1 Generalisation and rigid parameters

At let and top-level declarations, free dimension variables that do not occur in the environment are universally quantified and may appear anywhere in a type:

fn mean<d>(xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>): Real[d] = sum(xs) / length(xs)

fn map_quantity<d, e>(f: Real[d] -> Real[e], xs: Tensor<1, Real[d]>)
    : Tensor<1, Real[e]> = map(f, xs)

Declared dimension parameters are rigid. fn f<d> declares that f works for any d, and the body cannot choose it for the caller. After checking the body, every declared dimension parameter MUST still consist of free variables only (no base dimensions such as L or T), and the exponent vectors of the declared parameters MUST be linearly independent (they can still be chosen independently). A violation is E0318:

fn h<d>(x: Real[d]): Real[d] = x + 1 m
# error[E0318]: `h` is less generic than its declared type parameters
#   = the body requires `d = L`

fn g<d, e>(x: Real[d], y: Real[e]): Real[d] = x + y
#   = the body determines `e` from `d`

The unifier may write one variable as a product of other free variables (in linear_fit of §11.8 d is solved as d26*d28*d30/(d25*d29)); that is still generic: the test is independence, not "is it still a bare variable". A declared type parameter may be refined to a representation-polymorphic numeric type (fn number<a>(): a = 1 holds for all four representations) and is E0318 only when the body fixes it to a variable-free type; a declared Nat parameter is solved by equations per §3.5.1 and is not subject to this rule.

4.4.2 Weak variables

The weak variables introduced by symbol, symfun and the empty tensor are not generalised (§3.3.3):

let x = symbol("x")       # x : Real[d], d weak
let f = fn (t) => x * t   # d unifies with the dimension of t

If x is still unconstrained at the end of the declaration it defaults to 1 with warning W0301. Weak variables guarantee that one symbol has one dimension at every use.

4.4.3 Instances and erasure

instance Show for Real[d] covers every dimension; also defining Show for Real[L] would overlap (E0311). Dimension parameters are erased at run time: mean<L> and mean<T> are the same code, so no monomorphisation is needed and there is no run-time branching on a dimension; use Dyn when a run-time dimension is needed.

4.5 Dimension rules for operators

4.5.1 Scalars

With ρ = join(ρ1,ρ2) (§3.4):

Expression Result dimension Constraint
a + b, a - b, min, max, mod, rem, hypot, clamp d operands share d
a * b / a / b d1*d2 / d1/d2 —
a ^ n (n a literal) d^n n ∈ Q
a ^ n (n : Int at run time) not statically expressible d = 1 or Dyn (§3.4.3)
-a, abs, floor, ceil, round, trunc d —
sign(a) 1 —
atan2(y,x) 1 same d
sqrt(a), cbrt(a) d^(1/2), d^(1/3) —
exp ln log sin cos … 1 argument has d = 1

4.5.2 Tensors

The dimension rules for tensor reductions and linear algebra are in §3.5.5. fft and ifft keep the element dimension. prod and det accept dimensionless elements only and use Dyn otherwise (E0410). mean does not change the dimension, because its divisor is a dimensionless count.

4.5.3 Calculus

Expression Type
diff(f) (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> (Real[d] -> Real[e/d])
antiderivative(f) (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> (Real[d] -> Real[e*d])
defint(f,a,b) (Real[d] -> Real[e], Real[d], Real[d]) -> Result<Real[e*d], MathError>
limit(f,x) (Real[d] -> Real[e], Real[d]) -> Result<Real[e], MathError>
sum(f,a,b) (Real[1] -> Real[d], Real[1], Real[1]) -> Real[d]
product(f,a,b) only d = 1, or Dyn<Real> (the dimension is d^n)
solve(f) (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> Result<Solutions<Real,d>, MathError>
nsolve(f,x0) (Real[d] -> Real[e], Real[d]) -> Result<Real[d], MathError>
odeint(f,y0,ts) see §8.9

Differentiating f : Real[L] -> Real[T] gives Real[T/L] ("seconds per metre"), which prevents the derivative from being used with the dimension of the original function.

4.5.4 Statistics

mean, median and quantile keep the element dimension d; var gives d^2; std gives d; cov(xs,ys) gives d*e; corr, skewness and kurtosis are dimensionless; histogram(xs, n) has dimensionless counts and edges of dimension d.

4.5.5 Containers

Solutions<Real,d>, Option<Real[d]>, Result<Real[d],e> and IO (Real[d]) do not change the inner dimension. The result dimension of map is decided by the function; filter does not change it.

4.6 Dimensions of unit values

A unit is a value of type Unit[d]: m : Unit[L], km : Unit[L], degC : Unit[Θ] (affine, §5.6).

u * v : Unit[d*e]       u / v : Unit[d/e]       u ^ n : Unit[d^n]   # n a literal
Scalar<ρ,d> * Unit[e] : Scalar<ρ,d*e>
Scalar<ρ,d> / Unit[e] : Scalar<ρ,d/e>
Scalar<ρ,d> / Unit[d] : Scalar<ρ,1>     # the number counted in that unit

Tensors work element-wise in the same way. x / m gives "the number of metres" (Real[1]) and x * m gives a quantity. This is more basic than to(m, x): to changes only the display unit, whereas / really cancels the dimension.

4.7 Zero and infinity

0 is not dimension-polymorphic. It is an ordinary integer literal and defaults to Int:

0      : Int           0.0    : Float[1]
0 m    : Real[L]       0.0 m  : Float[L]
  • 0 + x is E0406 unless x : Int; to add a zero length write 0 m + x.
  • 0 * x promotes the Int to the representation of x and has dimension dim(x); 0 / x has dimension 1/dim(x).
  • x / 0 is a run-time E0801 for exact values and IEEE Inf/NaN for floats. (0 m)^2 : Real[L^2].

This gives up the convenience of 0 + 3 m in return for a simple, reliable type system; the error message suggests writing 0 m.

oo is the infinity of Real[1] and is not dimension-polymorphic; nan is Float[1]. A dimensioned infinity is written oo * 1 m. oo - oo and oo * 0 are nan; these are numeric semantics and do not affect typing.

4.8 Dimension inference

Implementation has three steps: constraint generation (walk the AST; literals, variables, operators, calls, records, tensors and patterns all produce constraints); unification (solve type, dimension and row variables and class constraints, using §4.3 for dimensions); and defaulting and generalisation (generalise at let boundaries, default what remains at the end of a declaration, check class constraints, export the interface). Constraint forms: τ1 ~ τ2 (types), δ1 ~ δ2 (dimensions, equivalent to δ1/δ2 ~ 1), ρ1 ~ ρ2 (rows) and C τ (class).

4.8.1 A complete derivation

fn fall_time(h: Float[L]): Float[T] =
    sqrt(2 * h / g)

h : Float[L] and g : Float[L/T^2]; 2 : Int is promoted to Float[1]; 2 * h : Float[L]; 2 * h / g : Float[L / (L/T^2)] = Float[T^2]; sqrt halves the exponent to give Float[T], which unifies with the return type. Written as sqrt(2 * h * g) the result is Float[L/T], which does not match Float[T], and the error is E0401.

4.8.2 Error messages

A dimension error MUST contain the canonical forms of the expected and actual dimensions, the difference (which base dimensions are extra or missing), the smallest offending subtree, and a fix when one can be inferred from context:

error[E0401]: dimension mismatch
  --> kinematics.aev:7:13
   |
 7 |     sqrt(2 * h * g)
   |              ^^^^^ expected Real[T^2], found Real[L^2/T^2]
   |
   = left:  L^2/T^2
   = right: T^2
   = difference: L^2/T^4
help: did you mean `2 * h / g`?

4.9 Dynamic dimensions Dyn

4.9.1 Purpose

Static dimensions cannot express: x^n (n : Int at run time, x dimensioned), det(A) (order n known at run time), product(f, a, b) (run-time term count), quantities read from CSV/JSON, and user-entered formulas (dimension is data). Dyn gathers them into one explicit, run-time-checked type.

4.9.2 Representation

Dyn        : Rep -> Type
DimValue   : Type        # run-time dimension: base dimensions with rational exponents
dyn        : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Dyn<ρ>                       # d must be concrete
check      : Dyn<ρ> -> Result<Scalar<ρ,d>, DimError>     # d must be concrete
dyn_with   : (Scalar<ρ,d>, DimValue) -> Dyn<ρ>
check_with : (DimValue, Dyn<ρ>) -> Result<Scalar<ρ,1>, DimError>
dim_of     : Dyn<ρ> -> DimValue
value_of   : Dyn<ρ> -> Scalar<ρ,1>                       # the number in coherent SI units
pow_dyn    : (Dyn<ρ>, Int) -> Dyn<ρ>
det_unit   : (Tensor<2, Scalar<ρ,d>>, Unit[d]) -> Dyn<ρ>

Internally a Dyn<ρ> is (a number, a DimValue, a display unit). It is not a Scalar and does not convert implicitly to Scalar<ρ,d>; it must be checked. A DimValue stores a canonical dimension text that can be restored losslessly: custom host bases, nominal owners and variables all have explicit encodings and SI text keeps its existing form. dim_of → dyn_with/check_with does not recover identity from display names, and the readable aliases used in diagnostics and numeric suffixes must not be taken as evidence of cross-module dimension identity.

4.9.3 Operations

+ and - require equal run-time dimensions; * and / add and subtract the dimension exponents; ^ Int multiplies the exponents; sqrt halves them. A dimension mismatch is the run-time error E0412: Dyn is already the escape layer, so the error ends evaluation and is not a Result. For a recoverable check, use check/check_with, which return a Result.

4.9.4 The boundary with the static layer

  • dyn(x) and check(x) are type-directed primitives: d MUST be a concrete dimension (no free variables) and the compiler materialises it as a DimValue. The result type of check must be fixed by an annotation or later use, otherwise it is E0304.
  • In generic code d is a variable and cannot be passed to dyn/check directly; use dyn_with(x, dim)/check_with(dim, x) and pass the run-time dimension explicitly (from a CSV schema, user input, and so on).
  • A Dyn<ρ> can be printed, compared (same dimension) and stored in containers, but it cannot take part in arithmetic that needs a Scalar<ρ,d>, and it is slower than Real/Float; do not use it in a hot loop.

4.9.5 Example

open Units.SI

fn scale_law(x: Real[L], n: Int): Dyn<Real> =
    pow_dyn(dyn(x), n)

fn volume(a: Tensor<2, Float[L]>): Dyn<Float> =
    det_unit(a, m)

fn main(): IO Unit = do
    println(scale_law(2 m, 3))                                   # 8 m^3
    println(volume([[2.0 m, 0.0 m], [0.0 m, 3.0 m]]))            # 6.0 m^2
    let r: Result<Real[L^3], DimError> = check(scale_law(2 m, 3))
    println(r)                                                   # Ok(8 m^3)

det_unit embodies a pattern: factor the unit out, compute dimensionlessly, and use Dyn to put the dimension back. This is more honest than letting det return an unknowable static dimension.

4.10 Relation to rssn's dimension layer

rssn's graph::dim is a term-level dimension inference: every scalar term has a dimension, every operator has a DimRule, dimension is an e-class attribute, and it supports rational and symbolic exponents, dimension variables and Advisory/Strict modes. Aevia's static layer shares the same base dimensions and exponent algebra but by default does not pass dimensions to rssn: the term graph of the static path is dimensionless, and the term-level layer is used only at FFI/export (with quantity) or for optional diagnostics.

Aevia static layer rssn term layer
When compile time run time / simplification
Subject types of the source program e-classes of the term graph
Exponents rational constants only rational + symbolic
Failure rejects the program diagnostic / error in strict mode

Both sides use one dimension algebra (aevia-dim) with the same base names and canonical forms. At start-up the bridge MUST check consistency item by item over the standard base dimensions, common derived dimensions and the unit table; on any disagreement Aevia's table wins and a warning is logged. rssn's DimExpr::to_term can intern Length^n as an atom, so the Dyn escape path does not fail at the bridge. This does not change the language decision: a static type position accepts rational exponents only, and a symbolic exponent can occur only in the run-time dimension of a Dyn.

4.11 Dimension errors and warnings

Code Situation
E0401 dimension mismatch (addition, subtraction, comparison, function argument)
E0402 dimension used as an exponent (2^(3 m))
E0403 transcendental function applied to a dimensioned argument (sin(3 m))
E0404 run-time exponent on a dimensioned base (x^n, n : Int)
E0405 illegal affine-point operation (Point + Point)
E0406 a bare 0 where a dimension is needed; write 0 m
E0407 unknown dimension or alias
E0408 reserved (unsatisfiable dimension constraint)
E0409 reserved (occurs check, §4.3.1)
E0410 dimensioned det/prod/product needs Dyn
E0411 incompatible element types in a tensor operation
E0412 Dyn run-time dimension check failed (run-time error)
E0318 a declared dimension parameter is more general than the body (§4.4.1)

4.12 Design trade-offs

  • Dimensions are types, not run-time tags. Run-time tags (Pint, Unitful) catch errors but leave them to testing or production; types catch them at compile time and guide inference (the dimension of mean follows its input). The cost is a more complex type system and the need to provide Dyn for what static types cannot express.
  • Units are not in types. If m and ft were different types, Real[L] would become Real[L, m], signatures would have to quantify over units, and type equality would get complicated. Reducing units to compile-time scales makes type equality equal dimension equality, lets the unit table grow independently and folds conversions at compile time; the price is that the type level does not distinguish different units of one dimension — and the real error in scientific computing is m + s.
  • No symbolic exponents. L^n needs the type level to carry an expression in n, turning unification into polynomial equations that may be undecidable. rssn can return a "symbolic constraint" at the term level instead of rejecting; Aevia chooses static conservatism plus the Dyn escape: the static path is decidable and explainable, the dynamic path explicit and checkable.
  • Angle = 1. In physical formulas the arguments of sin(ω t) and exp(i θ) are already dimensionless; making angles dimensionless with rad/deg for input and output is SI's choice and also the least obstructive.

The next chapter turns Unit[d] from a type into a usable unit table: prefixes, conversions, affine points and constants.

05 — Units

Chapter 04 made dimensions types; this chapter makes units compile-time scales. A unit is not part of a type. It supplies:

  1. a dimension d;
  2. a scale s (an exact multiple of the coherent SI unit);
  3. an affine offset o : Real[d] (affine units only).

The unit literal 3.2 km folds at compile time to 3200.0 (the coherent SI value) with type Float[L]; km does not stay in the value. To display a value in a unit, attach a display unit with to(km, x) (§5.4).

5.1 Unit declarations

dimension Currency                  # a user-defined base dimension
unit USD : Currency = 1             # base unit
unit EUR : Currency = 1.08 USD      # derived unit

unit m : Length = 1 prefixable      # base unit, SI prefixes allowed
unit km : L = 1000 m                # explicit derived unit
unit N : M*L/T^2 = 1 kg*m/s^2
unit degC : Θ = 1 K offset 273.15 K # affine unit

5.1.1 Syntax

dimension_decl ::= [ "private" ] "dimension" UIDENT [ "=" dim ]
unit_decl      ::= [ "private" ] "unit" unit_name ":" dim "=" unit_rhs [ "prefixable" ]
unit_rhs       ::= unit_scale [ unit_expr ] [ "offset" unit_scale unit_expr ]
unit_scale     ::= number [ "/" number ] | "pi" [ "/" number ]
  • dimension and unit are exported by default; private limits them to the module. Exported items enter the manifest and the registry, so downstream packages can import them. Units.* is a generated view of manifest.units; user declarations need no change to the standard library.
  • unit_name is an identifier or a special name containing ° (°C, °F).
  • unit_scale is a positive rational or a rational multiple of pi. Decimal literals are read as exact decimals (0.3048 is 381/1250), not as Float, so conversions such as ft → m are exact. Unit declarations are evaluated at compile time and admit no run-time expression; write a decimal literal of sufficient precision for an approximate scale.
  • An omitted unit_expr (§5.2) declares a base unit: the scale MUST be 1 and the dimension MUST be a base dimension.
  • offset is for affine units only; the offset's dimension MUST equal the unit's (E0504).
  • prefixable generates the SI-prefixed variants (§5.1.4).

5.1.2 Base and derived units

  • A unit name MUST be unique; a repeated declaration is E0505. A dimension may have several units of scale 1; they are equivalent.
  • Units are visible in declaration order: using a unit before its declaration is E0501, so definitions cannot form a cycle.
  • A derived scale is computed at compile time as an exact rational. If an exponent leaves the scale without an exact value (3 ft^(1/2): the scale of ft is not a perfect square), the error is E0506.
  • Unit declarations can be imported across modules; a private unit is not exported.

5.1.3 Unit namespace

Unit names and value names live in separate namespaces:

  • In the literal 3 m, m resolves in the unit namespace; let m = 5 does not change 3 m. To multiply by a variable, write 3 * m.
  • In an ordinary expression a unit name is the value Unit[d] (globally visible).
  • On a clash, the longest match wins: ms is milliseconds, not m*s; write m*s for metre-seconds.
  • A unit value cannot be accessed by a qualified name (Units.SI.km is invalid); units are globally visible from the kernel table, so use the short name.

Unit names and dimension names are also separate namespaces; one spelling may have a meaning in each, and the parser decides by position:

Spelling As a unit As a dimension
L litre (alias of liter/litre, prefixable) display alias of Length
T tesla display alias of Time
G gauss (1e-4 T) not the gravitational constant
h hour not Planck's constant

Canonical names are always the full names (Length/Mass/Time/…); single letters are for source and display only.

5.1.4 Prefixes

Symbol Factor Symbol Factor Symbol Factor Symbol Factor
Q quetta 10^30 P peta 10^15 h hecto 10^2 µ micro 10^-6
R ronna 10^27 T tera 10^12 da deca 10^1 n nano 10^-9
Y yotta 10^24 G giga 10^9 d deci 10^-1 p pico 10^-12
Z zetta 10^21 M mega 10^6 c centi 10^-2 f femto 10^-15
E exa 10^18 k kilo 10^3 m milli 10^-3 a atto 10^-18
z/y/r/q 10^-21/-24/-27/-30
  • A prefix applies only to a prefixable unit. kg is the SI base unit but prefixes attach to g (1/1000 kg, prefixable); an exact name match wins over prefix decomposition, so kg is the base unit, not k+g.
  • Longest match: dam is da+m, not d+am.
  • Prefixes do not stack: kkm is not a unit and is reported as E0501.
  • Binary prefixes Ki, Mi, Gi, Ti apply only to information units (bit, B).

5.2 Unit expressions

unit_expr   ::= unit_factor { ("*" | "·" | "/") unit_factor }
unit_factor ::= unit_atom [ "^" exponent ]
unit_atom   ::= unit_name | "(" unit_expr ")" | "%"
  • * and · are synonyms; / is left-associative. An exponent is an integer literal or a parenthesised rational: m^2, s^-1, m^(1/2).
  • Numbers (other than exponents) and juxtaposition by whitespace are not allowed inside a unit expression.
  • An affine unit may appear only alone; in a product or power it is E0508 (§5.6.4).

5.2.1 Unit values

At the value level a unit expression is a Unit[d] value:

let speed_unit = km / h          # Unit[L/T], scale 1000/3600
let v = 100 * speed_unit         # Real[L/T], value 250/9

100 speed_unit is not a unit literal (speed_unit is not a unit name) and is E0501; write 100 * speed_unit. Unit[d] supports u * v : Unit[d*e], u / v : Unit[d/e] and u ^ n : Unit[d^n] (n a literal); units can be compared (same dimension), printed and passed as arguments. A unit is not a number and cannot be added to a Real.

5.2.2 Evaluating a unit literal

value = number * scale(unit_expr) + offset(unit_expr)
type  = Scalar<rep(number), dim(unit_expr)>
  • A linear unit has offset = 0: 3 km → 3000, Real[L].
  • An affine unit is legal only alone: 25 °C → 25 + 273.15 = 298.15, Point[Θ] (§5.6).
  • An Int number gives Real (exact) by default and a Float number gives Float: 3 km : Real[L], 3.0 km : Float[L]. The expected type overrides the representation: let x: Real[L] = 3.0 m is the exact 3; in fall_time(20 m), 20 m is instantiated at Float[L].
  • A scale containing pi may give a symbolic result: 30 deg → pi/6 : Real[1], or 0.5235987755982988 when Float is expected.

5.3 Unit tables

The standard library Units.SI provides the following units (excerpt).

5.3.4 Unit modules

Units.SI base units, derived units, prefixes, common non-SI units
Units.Imperial imperial length, mass, volume, pressure, temperature
