Status: all of these modules can be opened. Their contents are generated from the kernel manifest (manifest.units, manifest.constants, manifest.unit_systems); every UnitValue carries aliases, prefixable, category and since. Units are session-private: a unit added with Session::register_unit appears only in that session's manifest.

5.4 Conversion and display

5.4.1 Three operations

let d = 3.2 km              # Float[L], value 3200.0
let e = to(m, d)            # value 3200.0, display unit m
let f = value_in(km, d)     # Float[1], value 3.2
let g = base(d)             # value 3200.0, display unit removed
  • to(u, x): x : Scalar<ρ,d> and u : Unit[d]; value and type are unchanged, only a display unit is attached.
  • value_in(u, x) is x / u, a dimensionless number.
  • base(x) removes the display unit and returns the coherent SI value.
  • A unit whose dimension differs from the value's is E0509.
  • An affine unit accepts only Point[d]: value_in(°C, point(298.15, K)) is 25. Using an affine unit on a temperature difference (Scalar<ρ,Θ>) is E0510, so value_in(°C, 298.15 K) is an error. Display a difference with delta_to(°C, dT), which uses the scale and ignores the offset.

Status: delta_to(°C, 10.0 K) currently prints -263.15 °C (the offset is still applied), which contradicts the semantics above. This is a known defect.

5.4.2 Display-unit propagation

At run time each scalar may carry an optional display unit:

  • x + y, x - y: kept if both sides have the same display unit, otherwise dropped to SI.
  • x * y, x / y: if one operand is dimensionless and carries a display unit, the other operand's display is kept; otherwise it is dropped.
  • to sets it explicitly and base clears it; function calls drop it by default (shape- preserving functions such as abs keep it).

A display unit is metadata: it takes no part in equality, typing or serialisation. 3.2 km == 3200 m is true.

Status: the warning W0501 (display unit dropped) has no emission site yet.

5.4.3 Printing

  • With a display unit, the number is converted to that unit (3.2 km, 25 °C).
  • Without one, a "best unit" is chosen: an SI derived unit (N, J, Pa) if one fits, otherwise a product of base units (kg*m/s^2). Fractional exponents print correctly (T^(1/2) → s^(1/2)).
  • show is guaranteed readable; repr is guaranteed to parse back to the same value.
  • Float printing uses the shortest round-trip representation; an exact Real prints as a fraction or radical.

5.5 Angles and dimensionless units

  • rad is a dimensionless unit of scale 1; deg/° has scale pi/180.
  • sin(30 deg) and sin(30) (30 radians) are both legal, because sin only requires a dimensionless argument; the type system does not tell radians from plain numbers.
  • atan2(y, x) returns dimensionless radians; display degrees with to(deg, ...).

5.6 Affine units and Point

5.6.1 Problem and types

Temperature is used in two ways: an absolute temperature (a point of an affine space; 25 °C is 298.15 K) and a temperature difference (a vector that can be added and scaled). Conflating them makes 20 °C + 30 °C come out as a wrong 50 °C or 596.3 K.

Point[Θ]        absolute temperature (affine point)
Float[Θ]        temperature difference (vector)
  • Only affine unit literals produce Point[d]: 25 °C, 77 °F. A linear unit literal produces a difference: 25 K is a difference.
  • Write an absolute temperature in kelvin with point(298.15, K).
  • Point has only the Float representation; a symbolic temperature is a Real[Θ] difference.
  • A Point can be a tensor element (Tensor<[nx,ny], Point[Θ]> is a temperature field); element-wise operations follow the point rules. Average a field with centroid, not mean (§13.9.2).

5.6.2 Operations

p - q        : Float[d]        two points subtract to a difference
p + x, x + p : Point[d]        point plus difference
p - x        : Point[d]        point minus difference
lerp(p,q,t)  : Point[d]        affine combination, t : Float[1]
p + q, k * p, p / k, p / K   : error E0405
  • Point is comparable (p == q, p < q); min and max return a Point; centroid : Tensor<1,Point[d]> -> Point[d].
  • In p + x and p - x, x MUST be Float[Θ]. An exact Real[Θ] variable does not qualify (E0302; use to_float), but a literal 10 K is instantiated at the expected Float, so 25.0 °C + 10 K is legal.
  • to(°C, p) sets a display unit, value_in(°C, p) returns the Celsius number, and base(p) returns the kelvin value as Float[Θ].
point     : (Float[1], Unit[d]) -> Point[d]
to        : (Unit[d], Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>      | (Unit[d], Point[d]) -> Point[d]
value_in  : (Unit[d], Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,1>      # E0510 for an affine unit
value_in  : (Unit[d], Point[d]) -> Float[1]
base      : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>                 | Point[d] -> Float[d]
delta_to  : (Unit[d], Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>      # scale only, no offset
unit_pow_dyn : (Unit[d], Int) -> Dyn<Real>             # run-time exponent
lerp      : (Point[d], Point[d], Float[1]) -> Point[d]

point(25.0, °C) is 298.15 K; the first argument is the numeric coefficient in that unit.

5.6.3 Why kelvin is linear

K is a linear unit (scale 1, offset 0), so 300 K is a difference by type: the kelvin scale serves both absolute temperatures and differences, and the unit alone cannot tell them apart. Celsius and Fahrenheit are affine scales, so their literals are naturally points.

let T1 = 25.0 °C            # Point[Θ]
let T2 = point(300.0, K)    # Point[Θ]
let dT = T2 - T1            # Float[Θ]
let T3 = T1 + 10.0 K        # Point[Θ]

5.6.4 Affine units in unit expressions

°C/s and °C*m are meaningless and the parser rejects them (E0508). °C may appear only alone as a literal unit, or as an argument of to/value_in.

5.7 Implementation of conversion

  • A scale is folded at compile time into an exact rational (or a symbolic value containing pi); 3 km directly yields 3000.
  • At run time only the display unit's name and conversion data remain: to(u, x) sets the display unit and printing uses x / u.scale. Affine conversion is value_in(°C, p) = (p - 273.15 K) / 1 K.
  • Unit equality: same dimension and same scale (and, for affine units, same offset). m and 100 cm are different unit values of the same dimension; to converts between them.

5.8 Physical constants

Units.Constants is generated from manifest.constants; symbols are identical to RSSN's:

Kind Constants (SI unit)
exact c (m/s), g_n (m/s^2), h, hbar (J*s), k_B (J/K), N_A (1/mol), e (C), F (C/mol), R (J/(mol*K)), M_u (kg/mol), T_tp (K), atm (Pa), G_0 (S), K_J (Hz/V), R_K (ohm), Phi_0 (Wb), K_cd (lm/W), dnu_Cs (Hz)
measured G (m^3/(kg*s^2)), alpha (1), R_inf (1/m), a_0 (m), epsilon_0 (F/m), mu_0 (N/A^2), mu_B (J/T), m_e, m_p, m_n, m_u (kg), sigma_SB (W/(m^2*K^4))
  • An exact constant is a defining value or an exact derivation of one and has no uncertainty; a measured constant is a CODATA 2022 value with a standard uncertainty. Both are exact rationals in the manifest (ScaleValue); exact means "defining value", not a storage type.
  • A constant is not a unit: it has a dimension and takes part in arithmetic, but cannot appear in a unit position. The core language carries no uncertainty; use Measurement (§8.14) when needed.
  • pi, e (Euler's number) and i live in Math. Units.Constants.e is the elementary charge and shares its name with Math.e; qualify it when both are open.

Metadata of each entry:

type ConstantInfo = {
    name: String,              # "speed of light in vacuum"
    symbol: String,            # "c"
    value: Scalar<Real, d>,    # coherent SI value
    dimension: Dim,
    unit: String,              # "m/s"
    exact: Bool,
    uncertainty: Option<Scalar<Float, d>>,
    source: String,
}

5.9 Unit errors

Code Situation
E0501 unknown unit name (including a stacked prefix such as kkm, or use before declaration)
E0502 unit not imported/opened (reserved)
E0503 unit cannot take a prefix (reserved)
E0504 affine offset dimension mismatch
E0505 duplicate unit name
E0506 scale has no exact value for the exponent
E0507 prefixed unit conflicts with an explicit unit (reserved)
E0508 affine unit in a product or power
E0509 unit dimension of to/value_in does not match the value
E0510 affine display of a temperature difference
E0511/E0512 unit-system errors (§5.11)

Status: E0502, E0503 and E0507 are reserved and currently unreachable. The unit table is the whole kernel table plus local declarations and has no module-level unit namespace, so the states "not imported", "prefix forbidden" and "prefix/explicit conflict" do not exist; the compiler does not substitute another error for them.

5.10 Relation to rssn's unit layer

  1. Single source of truth. Units.* is a generated view of manifest.units; user dimension/unit declarations register in the same registry and flow back to the manifest. UnitRegistry serves only run-time services such as resolve and format.
  2. The term graph carries no units. Real/Float values are coherent SI numbers on the host side. Only for rssn typesetting or export is a value wrapped with quantity(v, "<coherent SI unit>"); it never enters compute.
  3. Affine units are resolved in the bridge. A Point becomes a kelvin Float before reaching rssn; to(°C, ...) happens only in the display layer.
  4. Registry round trip. Only for purely rational exponents is the exact round trip DimTerm → Dimension → DimTerm via Dimension::exact_factors supported; symbolic exponents do not round-trip and Dimension.factors text MUST NOT be parsed. UnitDef → scale/offset → UnitDef MUST round-trip exactly.
  5. DimensionMode::Strict is for FFI/export only. The static path's term graph is dimensionless and uses Off; the dimension of a Dyn<ρ> is held by the host as a DimTerm.

5.11 Unit systems

Physicists often set some constants to 1 (c = 1, ħ = 1). Aevia does this as a module-level dimension reduction: dimensions written in the module are simplified by the unit system, while every value remains an honest SI quantity.

5.11.1 Selecting a unit system

The module header selects one by identifier:

module Hep with units natural

The kernel provides four systems (manifest.unit_systems); any other name in the header is E0512, and the diagnostic lists the available names.

Name Quantities set to 1 Generator dimensions left after reduction
natural c, hbar, k_B, epsilon_0 (Heaviside–Lorentz) energy, amount of substance, luminous intensity
planck c, hbar, G, k_B, 4 pi epsilon_0 amount of substance, luminous intensity
atomic hbar, m_e, e, 4 pi epsilon_0 temperature, amount of substance, luminous intensity
geometrized c, G length, current, temperature, amount of substance, luminous intensity

The constant modules (Units.Natural and so on) declare with units <system> themselves; c, hbar and k_B are exactly 1 in Units.Natural as a result of the reduction.

5.11.2 Reduction

Let the dimensions of the quantities set to 1 be q₁ … q_k and the generator dimensions g₁ … g_m; together they form a rational basis of the seven SI base dimensions. Every SI dimension d decomposes uniquely as

d = Π qᵢ^λᵢ · Π gⱼ^μⱼ

R(d) = Π gⱼ^μⱼ is the representative of d. The system value of a quantity x of dimension d is x / Π Qᵢ^λᵢ (Qᵢ the exact SI value), an SI quantity of dimension R(d). Example: in natural, R(L) = R(T) = Energy^-1, and the system value of 1 m is 1 m / (ħ c) ≈ 5.07e24 J⁻¹. User base dimensions and dimension variables lie outside the space spanned by the SI bases and are left unchanged.

In a module with a unit system, the dimension annotations and unit literals written in that module are reduced before checking, so the parameters of fn f(x: Real[L]) and fn f(x: Real[T]) have the same type in natural.

5.11.3 Values are not reinterpreted

Reduction changes only the dimensions written, never a value: every quantity is still an SI quantity whose type is written with its representative dimension. Values cross the boundary of a module with a unit system without conversion; an imported binding keeps the SI type it has in its home module.

5.11.4 Units without an exact scale

Reducing a unit literal needs an exact scale. If some exponent is not an integer the scale is irrational; the unit has no exact scale in that system and the error is E0511 (typically C in natural, which needs (ε₀ħc)^(1/2)). Write the number in system units, or convert an SI value with to_system (§5.11.5).

5.11.5 Explicit conversion

to_system("natural", x)           # SI quantity x → system value of dimension R(d)
from_system<L>("natural", y)      # system value y → quantity of SI dimension L
  • The system name MUST be a string literal, otherwise E0302; an unknown name is E0512; an argument count other than 2 is E0315.
  • to_system requires the argument's dimension to be known at check time, otherwise E0304; the result has the same representation and dimension R(d).
  • from_system<D> MUST name the target SI dimension D (reduction forgets it: L and T are both Energy^-1), otherwise E0304; D is always read as SI. The argument's dimension MUST equal R(D), otherwise it is reported as an ordinary dimension mismatch.
  • The result keeps the argument's representation: Float/Complex multiply by a floating factor; exact Int/Real stay exact rationals when the factor is an exact scale without π, and otherwise become a symbolic term; they never silently become floats.

5.11.6 Display

In a module with a unit system, a quantity with no display unit of its own is shown in the system's generator dimensions: an inverse energy in natural shows as eV^-1, not s^2/(m^2*kg). This selects the display unit only; the value is unchanged. Records, variants and tuples are displayed field by field.

The next chapter defines evaluation: when a Real is an exact number, a symbolic term or a Float, and how tensors and IO run.

06 — Evaluation semantics

This chapter defines run-time behaviour: what values are, the order of evaluation, how symbolic and numeric values coexist, how tensors are represented, how IO executes and how errors are reported.

6.1 Evaluation strategy

Aevia is strict, call-by-value and left-to-right:

  • Function arguments are evaluated before the call; a binary operator evaluates its left operand first; the right-hand side of a let is evaluated immediately and then bound.
  • Record fields are evaluated in written order; tensor elements in row-major order.
  • if and match evaluate only the selected branch; && and || short-circuit when the condition is decidable.

Pure functions have no side effects, so the order of evaluation affects only resource use, not the result.

6.2 Values

v ::= () | true | false
    | n : Int | r : Real | f : Float | c : Complex | s : String
    | tensor(shape, storage) | sparse(format, shape, storage) | table(schema, columns)
    | device(cpu|gpu|distributed) | quantity(value, unit)
    | value(dynamic)                 # RSSN dynamic boundary
    | plugin(handle)
    | { l1 = v1, ..., ln = vn } | C(v1, ..., vn)
    | closure(env, params, body) | io(action) | set(elements)
    | Ok(v) | Err(v) | None | Some(v)

6.2.1 Scalar representation

Real    ::= Exact(Number) | Sym(TermId)
Float   ::= F64(f64)                     # strictly f64, never symbolic
Complex ::= C(Real, Real) | C64(Complex64) | Sym(TermId)
Int     ::= BigInt
  • Number is rssn's exact number (arbitrary-precision integer or rational); TermId is a handle to a term in the symbolic graph, which may contain free symbols.
  • Float has only the F64 form; a floating-point expression containing symbols is a Real. This keeps Float's IEEE semantics, Ord and JIT clean.
  • C(re, im) is an exact or symbolic complex; C64 comes from numeric kernels such as eig and fft; Sym is a symbolic complex term. The type Complex[d] does not distinguish exact from approximate; inspect with re/im when that matters.
  • A display unit is scalar metadata Option<UnitRef> and takes no part in equality.

6.2.2 Closures and IO actions

A closure captures its lexical environment. A recursive function is realised by a self-reference in the environment; semantically it is a value, not a special form. IO a is an action description: a thunk that takes a world token and returns a. The token is not exposed; the runtime supplies it when executing main. Actions can be composed, passed and stored, but only the runtime executes them.

6.3 Exact, floating-point and symbolic

6.3.1 The three phases

Phase Representation Example
exact Exact(Number), rationals only 1/3, 2
symbolic Sym(TermId), free symbols or unsimplified operators x^2 + 1, sin(1), sqrt(2)
floating F64 0.8414709848078965

A Real moves between exact and symbolic; a Float is always f64. approx turns a Real without free symbols into an f64.

6.3.2 Arithmetic dispatch

Exact(a) op Exact(b):  op closed on rationals, no domain error -> Exact(a op b); else Sym(build(op, a, b))
Sym(_) op _ or _ op Sym(_):  Sym(build(op, ...)), folded immediately when possible
F64 op F64, or F64 with Int:  Int is promoted to F64, computed under IEEE
F64 with Exact/Sym:           Real — the F64 is converted exactly to a rational, then Real rules apply
  • Mixing Real and Float yields Real (exact wins, warning W0302): 1/3 + 0.5 is 5/6 and symbolic information is kept.
  • Int with Float yields Float; an unannotated 1.0 + 2 is Float (the join of §3.4.1). For an exact reading, give a Real expected type (§3.10.3).
  • Int / Int and negative integer powers yield an exact rational, even when the value is an integer; a rational with denominator 1 is not an Int.
  • 0 / 0 and 1 / 0 in the exact phase are E0801 (reported at compile time for literals); in the floating phase they give NaN/Inf under IEEE.

6.3.3 Building symbolic terms

build(op, args) calls rssn's operator constructor (Session::call_value) and does not run the global rewrite engine, so x + 1 is an O(1) term construction. Only these boundaries trigger compute:

  • explicit simplify/eval;
  • heavy requests: diff, integrate, solve, limit, sum, product, series, dsolve;
  • approx.

6.3.4 Domains and type invariants

  • Symbolic functions of Real are formal: sqrt(-1), ln(-1) and asin(2) stay as symbolic terms and do not turn into complex numbers; approx reports E0806 for a result outside the domain. For complex results use complex_sqrt or Complex.
  • ln(0) = -oo, ln(oo) = oo, exp(oo) = oo, exp(-oo) = 0.
  • The bridge MUST disable rssn rules that simplify real-domain expressions to complex ones, or check the range before returning; breaking the range of Real is the internal error E0809.
  • Extended-real arithmetic in the exact phase is partial. Apart from the boundary values above, + - * / ^ with oo stay symbolic terms (the undefined forms oo - oo, oo*0, oo/oo, oo ^ 0 are kept too, and the sign of -oo is not lost); they are neither silently folded into a finite number nor reported as errors. For a finite answer compute in the floating phase (inf/nan, nan != nan, -0.0 == 0.0). An infinite exponent (0 ^ oo, oo ^ oo) requires a finite rational exponent and is E0402.

6.3.5 eval, simplify, approx

eval      : Real[d] -> Real[d]          # symbolic simplification, may stay symbolic
simplify  : Real[d] -> Real[d]          # alias of eval
approx    : Real[d] -> Result<Float[d], EvalError>
approx    : Float[d] -> Result<Float[d], EvalError>
  • eval runs rssn with Target::Symbolic and returns the simplest closed form or the original; it need not terminate and is bounded by the budget (§6.9.1).
  • approx runs with Target::Numeric and requires no free symbols, otherwise E0803. The default tolerance is 1e-12; write approx(x, tol) to change it.

6.3.6 Symbol identity and substitution

let x = symbol("x")       # a fresh symbol, display name x
let y = symbol("x")       # another symbol, same display name
  • Each symbol("x") creates a fresh symbol: the internal name is unique, the display name is shared; repr shows the internal id.
  • subst(x, v, e) substitutes by symbol identity (x MUST be a symbol value); subst_all([(x, 1), (y, 2)], e) substitutes in bulk.
  • Symbolic equality x == y is a Prop, not a Bool, and cannot drive if; use decide(x == y) : Option<Bool> or assuming.

6.4 Implementation semantics of calculus and equations

6.4.1 diff

diff     : (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> (Real[d] -> Real[e/d])
diff_at  : ((Real[d] -> Real[e]), Real[d]) -> Real[e/d]      # = diff(f)(x)
numeric_deriv : (Float[d] -> Float[e]) -> (Float[d] -> Float[e/d]) # numeric/AD, not symbolic

Evaluating diff(f): create a fresh symbol ξ : Real[d]; apply f to ξ to get a symbolic term t; compute diff(t, ξ) with Target::Symbolic to get t'; return the closure fn (x) => subst(ξ, x, t').

If f does something non-symbolic with the symbolic value (such as if ξ > 0: ξ > 0 is a Prop and if requires Bool), the error is E0302. Differentiate a piecewise function by splitting it explicitly (by_cases/assuming, §8.2).

6.4.2 antiderivative and defint

antiderivative : (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> (Real[d] -> Real[e*d])
defint         : (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> Real[d] -> Real[d] -> Result<Real[e*d], MathError>

antiderivative(f) likewise creates ξ, computes integral(t, ξ) and returns a closure; the constant of integration is taken to be 0. defint(f,a,b) returns Err on failure.

6.4.3 limit, series, sum, product

  • limit(f, a); series(f, a, n) returns an expression with O(...).
  • sum(f, a, b) uses an index symbol κ : Real[1] and builds sum(f(κ), κ, a, b).
  • product(f, a, b) requires a dimensionless f; otherwise use product_dyn, which returns Dyn<ρ>.

6.4.4 solve and nsolve

solve    : (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> Result<Solutions<Real,d>, MathError>
nsolve   : (Real[d] -> Real[e]) -> Real[d] -> Result<Real[d], MathError>
nsolve_f : (Float[d] -> Float[e]) -> Float[d] -> Result<Float[d], MathError>

solve(f) hands f(ξ) = 0 to rssn and converts the result to Solutions<Real,d> (roots, multiplicities, parameter conditions) rather than a Set, because symbolic roots have no total order. No solution gives an empty Solutions; failure to solve gives Err. nsolve is numeric root finding and nsolve_f uses the floating-point kernel.

6.4.5 dsolve and SymFun

let y : SymFun<T, L> = symfun("y")
let eq = diff(y)(t) + y(t) / tau == 0 m/s   # eq : Prop, both sides L/T
let sol = dsolve(eq, y, t)           # Result<(Real[T] -> Real[L]), MathError>, tau : Real[T]
  • SymFun<d,e> is an unknown-function symbol; applying it to a Real[d] builds a term (the kernel's undefined-function node apply(y, t), whose dimension is the codomain dimension the checker solved for that call site).
  • dsolve(eq, y, t[, conditions]): conditions such as [y(0) == 1] fix the integration constants; undetermined constants are written C1, C2.
  • If the answer is explicit, y(t) = F(t), the result is the function s ↦ F(s); implicit or parametric solutions, and equations with no closed form, give Err (carrying the kernel's rendering); the implementation does not guess. For numeric ODEs use odeint (§8.9).

6.5 Pattern matching

  • Arms are tried in order; the first match wins. Constructor patterns match by tag, record patterns by field (open), tuple patterns by position.
  • The tensor pattern [a, b, ..rest] matches only rank 1 with length at least 2; rest binds the remaining slice.
  • Literal patterns: Int, Bool, String; a floating-point literal is not a valid pattern.
  • A guard is evaluated after the pattern binds and MUST be a concrete Bool; a Prop is E0308.
  • A non-exhaustive match is E0309 at compile time, and a match never falls through at run time; an arm that can never be selected warns W0201.

Status: exhaustiveness and redundancy analysis is bounded recursion with a pattern nesting limit of 24; beyond it a pattern is treated as covered, which can only suppress a diagnostic, never produce a false one. Or-patterns and empty enums cannot be expressed in the current grammar; a match with zero arms on a type with no constructors counts as exhaustive.

6.6 Tensor runtime

6.6.1 Representation

TensorData {
    shape: Vec<usize>,          # row-major
    storage: F64(Vec<f64>) | C128(Vec<Complex64>) | Exact(Vec<Number>)
           | Boxed(Vec<Value>) | Bool(Vec<bool>),
    display: Option<UnitRef>,
}
  • Float uses F64, Complex uses C128, Int/Real use Exact or Boxed, Bool uses Bool; mixed elements are promoted to the widest storage at construction ([1, 2.0] is [1.0, 2.0]).
  • The rank is the length of shape and equals n in the static type Tensor<n, τ> (checked by a run-time assertion).
  • A display unit applies to the whole tensor.
  • Construction: a literal is checked recursively for rectangularity, its element types are unified and it is flattened; zeros/ones/fill allocate contiguous storage; range/linspace produce F64 or Exact; CSV/JSON input is parsed as Float and the caller annotates the dimension.

6.6.2 Broadcasting

Same-rank broadcasting: for each axis, equal sizes d1 == d2 give d, a size of 1 takes the other size, anything else is E0802. A scalar broadcasts to every element; mixed ranks are a compile error (§3.5.3) unless you reshape explicitly.

6.6.3 Indexing and slicing

  • A negative index -1 is the last element; out of range is E0802.
  • a[i] returns a sub-tensor view of rank one less; a[i..j] returns a view. Views share the buffer (safe under immutable semantics). reshape returns a view on contiguous memory and copies otherwise.
  • There are no writes: every "modification" returns a new tensor, done in place when the reference is unique. set(a, idx, v) is the functional update.
  • at(a, i) is rank-polymorphic indexing Tensor<n,τ> -> Tensor<n-1,τ> (n>=1).
  • a @ b dispatches on rank: 2×2 matmul, 2×1 matvec, 1×2 vecmat, 1×1 dot (§3.5.3.1).

6.6.3.1 Strides

In a[i:j:s], s is an integer expression (not a Float, and not dimensioned); a non-integer stride is E0302.

  • An omitted s is 1; a[i:j] and a[i..j] are the same node.
  • Omitted endpoints follow the direction of the stride: for s > 0, i defaults to 0 and j to the axis length; for s < 0, i defaults to the last index and j to before the first index. So xs[::-1] is reverse(xs) and xs[::-2] is xs[4], xs[2], xs[0].
  • A negative explicit endpoint counts from the end and is clamped to the valid range; the end is half-open: xs[4:0:-1] is xs[4]…xs[1].

The length is exact: ceil((b - a) / s) for s > 0 and ceil((a - b) / |s|) for s < 0. A slice whose first step already passes b is empty (xs[5:2:1], xs[0:3:-1]) and does not panic. s == 0 never advances: it is a static E0802 when the stride is a literal and a run-time E0802 otherwise.

A negative stride yields a view: same buffer, different offset, that axis reversed (View { strides, reversed }); a positive stride multiplies that axis's stride by s. So xs[::-1] and reverse(xs) share one buffer, and m[:, ::-1] flips only the second axis.

6.6.4 Linear algebra

  • matmul, solve, inv, eig, svd, qr, lu on F64/C128 use rssn's numeric kernels; small Exact/Boxed matrices use symbolic linear-algebra rules, and a large matrix that cannot be numericised returns Err or stays symbolic.
  • det accepts only dimensionless input; a dimensioned matrix uses det_unit, which returns Dyn<ρ> (E0410).
  • solve/inv of a singular matrix return Err(SingularMatrix); eig always returns Complex.

6.6.5 Reductions

  • Empty tensor: sum = 0, prod = 1; mean is NaN for Float and E0802 for Real; min/max are E0802.
  • sum(a, axis) keeps the rank (that axis has size 1); sum(a) returns a scalar. F64 uses pairwise/Kahan summation; Exact sums exactly.
  • var is the sample variance by default (divide by n-1); var(xs, population=true) divides by n; std = sqrt(var); norm is the 2-norm by default and norm(xs, p) supports 1, 2, ∞.

6.7 Records, variants and collections

  • Records are stored sorted by field name; an update yields a new record and unchanged fields are shared. A variant is (tag, payload).
  • Set<a> and Map<a,b> are persistent ordered containers requiring TotalOrd a: elements are ordered by total_compare, so iteration is deterministic, and Set's == is set equality. Float is not TotalOrd, so Set<Float> is invalid; wrap in TotalFloat.
  • Solutions<ρ,d> = { roots: Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>>, multiplicities: Tensor<1, Int>, conditions: Tensor<1, Condition> }; it needs no Ord.

6.8 IO

6.8.1 Execution model

The runtime executes main : IO Unit: initialise the standard library and modules (pure evaluation) → build main's action → execute it (print writes standard output, read_file reads a file, plot writes HTML) → the action returns (). An uncaught panic terminates and prints the call stack.

A do block saves its program and lexical environment when built and does not run the pure expressions inside it. On execution each statement is evaluated in source order: the next statement, including a following let, action arguments and string interpolation, is evaluated only after the previous IO action has completed; inserting an unused let does not change the order of effects. The same action can be replayed, and each run has its own bindings and execution position. The block's result is that of the last action; if the last statement is a let or <- binding, the block returns Unit.

? applies only to Result<T,E> or Option<T>. A failure in a pure function returns from that function; a failure inside do returns from the current IO program; a nested function has its own return boundary. The target MUST be the same container family and the Result error types MUST match; there is no implicit error conversion (the same applies to a function without a return annotation). So the do of an IO (Result<U,E>) can propagate a Result<T,E> while an IO Unit cannot. An early return skips the rest of the block, and the enclosing IO can still bind and handle the failure value.

6.8.2 Common actions

print    : Show a => a -> IO Unit
println  : Show a => a -> IO Unit
eprintln : Show a => a -> IO Unit
read_line: () -> IO String
read_file: String -> IO (Result<String, IOError>)
write_file: String * String -> IO (Result<(), IOError>)
read_csv_dyn : String -> IO (Result<Table<Dynamic>, DataError>)
plot     : PlotSpec -> IO Unit
time_now : () -> IO Float[T]
random   : () -> IO Float[1]

print uses show and println appends a newline. read_file returns a Result and throws no exception. plot writes plot.html in the current directory (or --out). random uses the run-time RNG; for reproducibility use the pure API rng(seed).

6.8.3 Purity

An IO action cannot appear in a pure function (E0313); a pure function may receive or return an action as a value but cannot execute it. There is no unsafePerformIO. main is the only entry point that runs automatically.

6.9 Budgets, errors and resources

6.9.1 Symbolic budget

rssn's compute takes a Budget. The actual defaults (rssn::graph::schedule::Budget::default) are max_iterations = 24, max_nodes = 20_000, match_limit = 1_000, reduce_rounds = 32, patience = 3, time_limit = None. Exhaustion is E0808; heavy requests (integrate/solve) return Err.

Status: there is no language-level with_budget(seconds, rewrites, thunk) yet. The CLI's --budget is the tail-call budget (§9.3.4), not the symbolic budget; the symbolic budget takes the kernel defaults and is adjusted through the kernel Config/Budget API (embedding layer).

6.9.2 Error classes

Code Situation Recoverable
E0801 exact division or modulo by zero no (floating point follows IEEE)
E0802 shape or index error no
E0803 approx met a free symbol yes (Result)
E0804 symbolic condition cannot be decided, or the assumption of assuming is false no
E0805 stack overflow (non-tail recursion too deep) no
E0806 domain error yes (Result)
E0807 numerical method did not converge yes (Result)
E0808 budget exhausted yes (Result/panic)
E0809 type invariant broken by a backend no (internal error)
E0810 IO error yes (Result)
E0811 task failure (§9.7.3) yes
E0899 panic no

6.9.3 Stack and tail calls

  • A self tail call uses a trampoline and constant stack.
  • Non-tail recursion has two bounds: the library default call-depth limit of 2048 (Interp::max_depth); and, under the aevia CLI, which runs the interpreter on a thread stack of --max-stack <MiB> (default 1024), a depth limit raised to 10⁷ and replaced in practice by the remaining stack (an eighth of the stack, 8–64 MiB, is kept in reserve). Exceeding either is E0805 with a call stack.
  • A non-terminating tail loop is bounded by the consecutive-tail-call budget: default 100,000,000 (Interp::max_tail_calls, changed by the CLI's --budget); exceeding it is E0805. This is runaway protection, not a semantic limit (§9.3.4).
  • Expression nesting depth is also bounded (default 16,384 nodes) and exceeding it is E0805. A panic prints the call stack.

6.10 Determinism and parallelism

  • Pure evaluation is fully deterministic (floating-point NaNs are canonicalised).
  • par_map(f, xs) preserves input order and the thread count does not affect the result; it requires Parallel a (only Int/Float and their tensors), and symbolic Real does not run in parallel. When a value cannot cross threads it falls back to sequential evaluation with the same result (§9.7.2). The parallelism of compute_all affects speed only.
  • Set/Map iteration order follows TotalOrd, not a hash seed. random() is IO; rng(seed) is pure and deterministic.

6.11 Module initialisation

  • Top-level lets are evaluated eagerly in dependency order; mutually recursive top-level functions bind their names first and then evaluate their closures.
  • symbols are created in source order, so internal ids are deterministic. Unit, type and class declarations produce no run-time value (units excepted).
  • main runs after all modules are initialised.

6.12 Equality, ordering and display

6.12.1 Equality

  • Concrete Int/Float values compare numerically; Float follows IEEE (NaN != NaN, -0.0 == 0.0).
  • == on Real returns Prop; for a concrete boolean use decide(x == y), for approximate equality use approx_eq.
  • Tensors: equal shape and element-wise equality (a Real tensor gives Prop); records and variants compare structurally; functions have no Eq; Set compares as a set.

6.12.2 Ordering

  • Int is totally ordered; String by Unicode code point; records and tuples lexicographically by field.
  • Float's compare returns Option<Ordering> (None for NaN; </> are false for NaN).
  • TotalOrd's total_compare sorts NaN last and serves Set/sort; Float itself is not TotalOrd.
  • Real and tensors have no Ord; for tensors use lexicographic or norm.

6.12.3 Display

  • show(x) is human-readable and includes the display unit; repr(x) parses back to the same value and shows internal ids of symbols; to_latex and to_typst typeset mathematics (single-letter symbols in italics, Greek names as letters, products with negative powers as \frac, elementary functions as upright operator names, exp(u) as e^{u}).
  • A float prints in its shortest round-trip form and always keeps its phase: an integer-valued Float has a decimal point (10.0, -0.0) and large or small magnitudes use an exponent (1e20, 1e-9); the exact 10 and the float 10.0 look different. An exact Real prints as a fraction or radical; a Complex prints as a + b*i with both parts following the Float rule.
  • IEEE infinities print as inf/-inf and NaN as nan; oo/-oo denote only the exact infinities of the Real phase. repr writes a float infinity as (1.0 / 0.0).
  • A string shown on its own is its text; inside a container it is quoted: show(Err("not found")) is Err("not found") and show(["a, b"]) is ["a, b"].
  • Converting a Float with a display unit rounds only once ((x - offset) / scale is computed on exact rationals, then the nearest f64 is taken): to(°C, point(300.0, K)) displays as 26.85 °C. The value itself is unchanged.
  • Sums print in descending degree (x^3 - 3*x + 1) and b^(1/2) prints as sqrt(b); terms returned by the kernel use Aevia's spelling (e, not E).

6.13 A worked example

let f = fn t => t^3 - 3*t + 1
let df = diff(f)
let a = df(2)          # 9, exact
let b = approx(a)      # Ok(9.0)

diff(f) creates ξ, applies f to get ξ^3 - 3ξ + 1, rssn simplifies the derivative to 3ξ^2 - 3 and a closure is returned; df(2) substitutes ξ=2 and computes 9 exactly; approx(9) gives Ok(9.0). If f were fn t => if t > 0 then t^2 else -t^2, t > 0 is a Prop and the error is E0302; for a symbolic piecewise function use by_cases, which builds an ite term, or use a numeric derivative at concrete points.

The next chapter defines modules, packages and visibility.

07 — Modules, packages and visibility

The module system is file-level, functor-free and free of implicit global state: a file is a module, and a package is a set of modules plus a manifest. Name resolution is complete at compile time; there is no dynamic import at run time.

7.1 Modules

7.1.1 A file is a module

src/
  kinematics.aev          -> module Kinematics
  thermo/
    ideal_gas.aev         -> module Thermo.IdealGas
    cycles.aev            -> module Thermo.Cycles
  • A module name is determined by the path relative to src/, in UpperCamelCase segments; it is case-sensitive, and a file holds exactly one module.
  • A file may write module Thermo.IdealGas. When imported, the declared name MUST match the source path, otherwise E0601. A package dependency's import alias belongs to the importer's namespace and does not rename the module at its definition (src/Model.aev of package Library may declare module Model or module Library.Model; a user importing it as Renamed.Model need not change it). An entry file run directly has no import-path context and is not checked.
  • Module lookup and execution respect the defining package's scope: import Helper inside a dependency finds that dependency's src/Helper.aev and is never overridden by an application module of the same name. Load nodes are de-duplicated by (canonical file, owning package); a cycle is reported on the full node as E0602.
  • import Pkg uses the entry of the dependency's own manifest.

7.1.2 Declaration order

Top-level declarations may refer to one another, including forward references and recursion: the compiler first collects all top-level names and signatures and then checks the bodies. Unit and dimension declarations may also come later; units from other modules are supplied by import.

fn f(x: Int): Int = g(x) + 1
fn g(x: Int): Int = x * 2

7.1.3 Exports

All top-level declarations are exported by default; private hides one:

private let internal_scale = 1.0e-3
private fn helper(x: Real[L]): Real[L] = x * internal_scale

fn public_api(x: Real[L]): Real[L] = helper(x)
  • private is allowed only on let, fn, type, dimension, unit and class, otherwise E0202; a private declaration is still visible inside its module.
  • The constructors of a variant type are exported with the type and cannot be made private individually; the constructors of a private type do not enter the public interface.
  • Instances are always exported with the module (separate compilation would otherwise lose them); there is no private instance (E0202).

7.2 Import and open

7.2.1 import

open Units.SI
import Math as M
import Data.Csv { read_csv, Table }
  • import M binds M as a qualified name, used as M.f; import M as N aliases it.
  • import M { f, g } is a selective open and equals open M { f, g }.
  • Imports are idempotent; repeating one is not an error.
  • A module that cannot be found is E0607, and the diagnostic gives the expected file path.

7.2.2 open

  • open M brings every exported name of M into scope unqualified; open M { f, g } brings only the listed names.
  • open affects only the current module and is not transitive (there is no re-export).
  • A local definition shadows an opened name. If two modules open the same name with different types, the error is E0603; the same type (one module opened twice) is not a clash.
  • import and open can bring in values (functions, constants, unit values), nominal variant constructors and transparent type aliases (after import A { Pair } or open Physics.Ising you can write Params); an alias is exported with the interface and expanded in the importer.

Units do not depend on imports. The kernel unit table is global: with no import or open, both 3 km and to(m, x) work (there is no module-level unit namespace, §5.9). A unit a user declares with unit is exported with its module. A unit value cannot be reached by the qualified name Units.SI.km.

7.2.3 Qualified names

Math.sqrt, Thermo.IdealGas.pressure: a qualified name starts at a module root; there are no relative imports. It is a lookup path and introduces no binding. Types, constructors and values can all be qualified, provided the module is imported first.

7.2.4 Name resolution order

An unqualified name x is looked up in:

  1. local bindings (parameters, let, patterns);
  2. the current module's top-level declarations;
  3. names the current module opens;
  4. the Prelude;
  5. otherwise E0301.

Earlier steps shadow later ones; a clash is E0603 only inside step 3.

7.3 Dependencies, interfaces and identity

  • The module dependency graph MUST be acyclic; a cyclic import is E0602. Type, class and instance dependencies are ordered too, and instance resolution needs the relevant modules already checked.
  • Each module exports an interface: types and constructors, value signatures, units, class declarations, instances and alias definitions. An alias MUST carry its expansion, or the importer cannot expand type Vec2 = { x: Float, y: Float } (exporting only the name gives E0301).
  • A module merges only the interfaces in its own dependency closure; it does not gain another module's interface because that module happened to be checked earlier. There are no implicit global instances: an instance is visible only from imported modules, and the standard library's common instances are imported by the Prelude.
  • An interface can be cached as a .aevi file; the invalidation key is the source hash, the dependency-interface hashes, the capability set and the compiler build (§9.11.3).
  • Nominal identity is symbolic. A class key is (defining module path, class name); an instance key adds the canonical head type (aliases expanded first); an ADT's identity is the canonical source directory of the defining package (with its name and version) plus the source path inside the package, and the readable module name is kept separately. Two local packages of the same name and version at different source directories therefore have different type and instance keys; two import aliases of one package share an identity. AdtId is only an in-process witness and does not enter interfaces, caches or cross-process identity.
  • Stand-alone sources and virtual sessions (the REPL, unsaved buffers) also have a stable source identity; identity uses no FileId, whole-file content hash or random nonce.
  • An ADT's declaration schema is the canonical encoding of the nominal type graph reachable from it (constructor names and order, expanded field types, parameter kinds, alpha-normalised dimension bindings); an unrelated source edit does not change it.
  • A private class and its local instances keep an exact run-time key but do not enter the public interface; a class method passed as a value or captured by a closure keeps its full ClassKey.
  • A public transparent alias carries its resolved template; a private helper alias is expanded at its definition, so consumers never see it.

7.4 Type-class instances and modules

  • Importing a module imports its classes, instances and aliases. Imported instances take part in global coherence checking (overlap is E0311, and the message names both modules) but are not re-checked for orphans.
  • Orphan rule: an instance MUST be defined in the module of the type or of the class, otherwise E0604; instances generated by derive follow the same rule.
  • A private class is visible only in its defining module, and its instances do not enter the interface.
  • At run time dispatch uses witnesses at quantified call sites only (§9.2.3); the checker MUST be able to prove that the witnesses of candidate instances are pairwise disjoint, otherwise it reports E0311/E0310 at check time. It never guesses from run-time shapes.

7.5 Packages

7.5.1 Manifest

aevia.toml:

[package]
name = "kinematics"
version = "0.1.0"
entry = "src/main.aev"      # optional; default src/main.aev

[dependencies]
Units = "^1.2"                                        # official scope: aevia/units
Plot = { package = "apich/plot", version = "~2.1" }   # registry package
Kinematics = { path = "../kinematics" }               # local path package
Dev = { path = "../dev", package = "apich/dev", version = "^0.3" }  # path for local work, version once published

Status: the manifest reader reads only [package] (keys name, version, entry) and [dependencies]. Other tables ([compat], [capabilities], [build], [profile.*]) and other [package] keys (edition, authors, description) are E0605. Description, licence, keywords and repository are supplied as options to aevia publish (§9.13). Capability declarations have no run-time permission check; only --caps exists (§9.14). There is no git dependency (E0605).

  • name is the package name; version is SemVer 2.0 major.minor.patch, optionally with -pre-release; a published version MUST NOT carry +build (two versions differing only in build metadata have equal precedence and the registry cannot tell them apart).
  • A dependency has one of three sources: registry, path, or both together. The registry is content-addressed and signed.
  • The entry MUST define main : IO Unit for aevia run. Without main the CLI reports this program has no main (no diagnostic code, exit code 1); a library package is only imported.

7.5.1.1 Dependency declarations and version requirements

A key of [dependencies] is an import root (UpperCamelCase, no -; otherwise E0605). Values take these forms:

Form Meaning
Units = "^1.2" an official-scope package aevia/units. The package name is the key in snake_case (UnitsExtra → aevia/units_extra); an acronym key (FFT) needs an explicit package
Plot = { package = "apich/plot", version = "~2.1" } a registry package named owner/name
Lib = { path = "../lib" } a local path package
Dev = { path = "../dev", package = "apich/dev", version = "^0.3" } both: path when building this package from source; only package/version when it is used as a registry package (the path directory does not exist). A published manifest MUST NOT contain a path-only dependency; the server rejects it at publish time (E_MANIFEST)

version is a version requirement (Cargo syntax and meaning):

Form Meaning
1.2.3, ^1.2.3 >=1.2.3, <2.0.0 (a bare version is ^)
^0.2.3 / ^0.0.3 >=0.2.3, <0.3.0 / exactly 0.0.3
~1.2.3, ~1.2, ~1 >=1.2.3, <1.3.0, <1.3.0, <2.0.0
1.*, 1.2.x wildcard
>=1.2, <2, =1.2.3, >1, <=1.2 comparators; a comma means conjunction
* any release version

A pre-release (1.0.0-rc.1) satisfies a requirement only if some comparator names a pre-release of the same major.minor.patch; ^1 and * never select a pre-release, so aevia update does not silently adopt a release candidate.

7.5.1.2 Resolution: at most one version per compatibility series

The resolver is aevia-resolve (a pure crate with no I/O, shared by the toolchain and the registry):

  1. It satisfies every requirement, the dependencies of each selected version are satisfied too, and there is no dependency cycle (a cycle is an error and is listed).
  2. At most one version per compatibility series of each package. The series is the major version for >=1.0 (1.x), 0.y for 0.y, and 0.0.z for 0.0.z. So 1.4 and 1.9 never appear together, while 1.x and 2.x can coexist with distinct nominal types (§7.3). Coexistence needs the two edges to use different import roots; the program-wide import-root table still maps one root name to one package, otherwise E0606 (the message names the two contending versions).
  3. A locked version is preferred; otherwise the highest compatible version is chosen. A yanked version can be selected only if the lock file already contains it.
  4. The result does not depend on index iteration order; when unsatisfiable, the explanation is derivation-style: which requirement could not be met, which candidates were tried and why each failed.
  5. The implementation is a backtracking solver with conflict-directed backjumping (not PubGrub, because one requirement can be satisfied by several series).

The index deps of a registry package records only package, req and, when it differs from the default import root, import.

7.5.2 Lock file

aevia.lock records each dependency's exact version, source and digest. A build MUST use the lock file; aevia update updates it, and under --locked any change is an error. Format (version = 1, fixed fields and order, packages sorted by (source, import), so an unchanged resolution yields a byte-identical file):

[[package]]
name = "apich/plot"          # registry package: registry identity owner/name
version = "2.1.3"
import = "Plot"              # import root (one entry per alias, as for path packages)
source = "registry+aevia"    # the registry's logical name, not a URL: a mirror change does not touch the lock
checksum = "sha256:…"        # archive digest from the index (the signature covers it)

[[package]]
name = "dep"                 # path package: [package] name
version = "0.2.0"
import = "Dep"
source = "path+../dep"
hash = "fnv1a64:…"           # digest of manifest + source tree + entry
  • Paths are relative to the root package; the digest of a path dependency covers the canonical manifest, src/**/*.aev and the entry file, so changing entry or a dependency edge also invalidates --locked; a symlink cycle in a source directory is E0607.
  • Index: .aevia/registry/index/<owner>/<name> (shareable through $AEVIA_REGISTRY_DIR) is the verbatim body of GET /api/v1/index/{owner}/{name}. Resolution reads only these files; when one is missing, aevia add/update/tree call aevia-pkg index to download it (forbidden by --offline). The compiler itself never touches the network.
  • Source: aevia fetch (= aevia-pkg vendor) downloads archives, verifies the digest and the OpenPGP/SSH signature, unpacks into .aevia/registry/src/<owner>/<name>/<version>/ and writes .aevia-pkg-checksum. A build MUST read only these directories and rejects a directory without the marker or with a marker that differs from the index digest (E0607), and a directory whose manifest dependencies disagree with the index record (E0605).
  • aevia update without arguments ignores the lock and takes the newest versions; aevia update owner/name… moves only the named packages. aevia tree prints the dependency tree. aevia clean keeps .aevia/registry; only aevia clean --registry removes it.
  • aevia add resolves the whole candidate graph before staging the manifest and lock; an ordinary I/O failure restores the original manifest, and a failed resolution rewrites nothing.

Status: the resolver lets incompatible series of one package coexist, but giving each package its own dependency namespace (a per-package Roots lookup) is not implemented, so coexistence requires distinct import roots.

7.5.3 Directory layout

kinematics/
  aevia.toml
  aevia.lock
  src/
    main.aev
    kinematics.aev
    thermo/ideal_gas.aev
  tests/fall_time.aev
  examples/projectile.aev
  docs/
  • Every .aev file under src/ is a module; main.aev is the default entry. aevia test compiles and runs tests/.
  • aevia run --example name resolves examples/<name>.aev by name (a name is not a path; one with a path separator is rejected) and lists the available examples when it finds none; --example and a file argument are mutually exclusive.
  • aevia doc writes Markdown API documentation, by default into docs/.

7.5.4 Tests

# tests/fall_time.aev
open Kinematics

test "fall time from 20 m" =
    approx_eq(fall_time(20.0 m), 2.019 s, 0.001 s)
  • A test declaration is a Bool expression; aevia test runs them all. approx_eq(a, b, tol) requires a, b and tol to have the same dimension.
  • To test a compile-time error use compile_fail("<source>") (§8.13): it hands a source fragment to the compiler and requires that compilation fail; only fragments without imports are supported at present. @compile_fail is not an attribute (an unknown attribute is E0210). Property tests use forall (§8.13).

7.5.5 Documentation

  • A ## doc comment attaches to the next declaration and contains Markdown; aevia doc extracts type signatures, units and examples, and reports undocumented declarations in the index.
  • Code blocks tagged aevia in doc comments are compile-checked: aevia test --doc [FILE…] extracts the fenced blocks and compiles each one; the first line of a block may be compile_fail or compile_fail EXXXX. A snippet compiles as its own synthetic module: the declaring module's import/open lines are copied in, but the module's own declarations do not enter the snippet implicitly; import the declaring module explicitly when needed. A failing dependency or inherited import does not satisfy compile_fail.

7.6 Versions and compatibility

  • edition selects syntax and semantics (the manifest does not read it yet, §7.5.1); libraries follow SemVer: a breaking change raises major, a new API raises minor, a fix raises patch.
  • A change to a type or dimension signature, to a unit's scale or offset, or adding or removing an instance is breaking; adding a unit is minor.

7.7 Module errors

Code Situation
E0601 module name does not match its path
E0602 cyclic import
E0603 two modules open the same name with different types
E0604 orphan instance
E0605 invalid package manifest (unknown table or key, non-SemVer version, git dependency, dependency key not a module name, lock and index disagree)
E0606 dependency version conflict (unsatisfiable requirement, dependency cycle, import-root contention)
E0607 module or package not found (including registry index not cached, package not fetched, checksum marker mismatch, symlink cycle)
E0608 the embedding API aevia_core::run got no runnable module (the CLI prints only a message when main is missing)

The next chapter gives the standard library's signatures.

08 — Standard library

The standard library is part of the language: it defines the Prelude, mathematical functions, units, linear algebra, calculus, statistics, data, plotting and simulation. This chapter gives the normative signatures. An implementation MAY add functions but MUST NOT change the type or dimension rule of an existing one.

In signatures, ρ is a numeric representation (Int, Real, Float, Complex), d and e are dimensions, n is a rank, and a and b are types. Scalar<ρ,d> is written Real[d] etc. in surface syntax.

8.1 Prelude

The Prelude is imported automatically; no open is needed.

8.1.1 Function composition

id       : a -> a
const    : (a, b) -> a
compose  : ((b -> c), (a -> b)) -> (a -> c)
flip     : ((a, b) -> c) -> ((b, a) -> c)
curry    : ((a, b) -> c) -> (a -> (b -> c))
uncurry  : ((a -> (b -> c))) -> ((a, b) -> c)
apply    : ((a -> b), a) -> b

compose(f, g)(x) = f(g(x)). |> and <| are syntax, not functions.

8.1.2 Booleans and comparison

not      : Bool -> Bool
xor      : (Bool, Bool) -> Bool
(&&)     : (Bool, Bool) -> Bool
(||)     : (Bool, Bool) -> Bool
(==)     : Eq a => (a, a) -> Bool
(!=)     : Eq a => (a, a) -> Bool
(<)      : Ord a => (a, a) -> Bool
(<=)     : Ord a => (a, a) -> Bool
(>)      : Ord a => (a, a) -> Bool
(>=)     : Ord a => (a, a) -> Bool
compare  : Ord a => (a, a) -> Option<Ordering>
partial_compare : (Float, Float) -> Option<Ordering>   # NaN -> None
total_compare   : TotalOrd a => (a, a) -> Ordering

sym_eq   : (Real[d], Real[d]) -> Prop
sym_ne   : (Real[d], Real[d]) -> Prop
sym_lt   : (Real[d], Real[d]) -> Prop
decide   : Prop -> Option<Bool>
assuming : (Prop, () -> a) -> a
by_cases : (Prop, () -> Real[d], () -> Real[d]) -> Real[d]

by_cases(p, yes, no) selects a branch when p is decidable; otherwise it builds a symbolic ite term (for symbolic work on piecewise functions). assuming supports unary predicates only (x > 0, x >= 0, x != 0, x ∈ ℤ).

Real has no Eq/Ord instances; its comparisons yield Prop. Int, Float and String comparisons yield Bool. TotalOrd is used by Set and sort; Float needs the TotalFloat wrapper.

8.1.3 Numbers

abs      : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>        # ρ ≠ Complex
sign     : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Int                 # ρ ≠ Complex
min      : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>   # ρ ≠ Complex
max      : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>   # ρ ≠ Complex
clamp    : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>
floor    : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>         # ρ ≠ Complex
ceil     : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>         # ρ ≠ Complex
round    : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>         # ρ ≠ Complex
round    : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Int) -> Scalar<ρ,d>   # keep n decimals; negative n rounds to tens, hundreds, ...
is_nan   : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Bool
is_finite : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Bool
is_infinite : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Bool
trunc    : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d>         # ρ ≠ Complex
mod      : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>   # ρ ≠ Complex
rem      : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>   # ρ ≠ Complex
div      : (Int, Int) -> Int
quot     : (Int, Int) -> Int
gcd      : (Int, Int) -> Int
lcm      : (Int, Int) -> Int
factorial: Int -> Int
to_float : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Result<Float[d], EvalError>   # fails for symbolic / free-symbol values
to_real  : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Real[d]
to_int   : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Int
zero     : Scalar<ρ,d>                 # zero of the representation/dimension, for fold/sum
rng      : Int -> (() -> Float[1])     # pure, deterministic; returns a "next number" function

min and max are binary; tensor reductions are min_all and max_all. zero is the start of fold((+), zero, xs); sum([]) internally uses the zero of the corresponding representation and dimension.

rng(seed) is a pure pseudo-random generator: the same seed gives the same sequence in a program, independent of evaluation order and thread count. It returns a nullary function that yields the next Float[1] on each call. random() (§6.8.2) is the IO version and is not reproducible.

8.1.4 Strings

length   : String -> Int
concat   : (String, String) -> String
join     : (String, Tensor<1, String>) -> String
split    : (String, String) -> Tensor<1, String>
replace  : (String, String, String) -> String
trim     : String -> String
upper    : String -> String
lower    : String -> String
contains : (String, String) -> Bool
starts_with : (String, String) -> Bool
ends_with   : (String, String) -> Bool
parse_int   : String -> Result<Int, ParseError>
parse_float : String -> Result<Float, ParseError>
format      : (String, Tensor<1, String>) -> String

String indexing counts grapheme clusters. length(s), chars(s), s[i] and s[i..j]/s[i:j:k] all work on grapheme clusters ("e\u{301}" is one position); s[i] and slices are String, negative indices count from the end, and out-of-range access is E0802. chars(s) returns Tensor<1, String>.

The placeholders of format(template, args) are {0}, {1} (positional) and {} (next argument); other brace content and placeholders beyond the argument count are kept as written. In an ordinary string literal {…} is interpolation, so templates are usually raw strings (r"{} m") or read from a file; an empty interpolation "{}" is E0104 and the diagnostic suggests both forms.

8.1.5 Containers

map      : ((a -> b), Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, b>
map2     : ((a, b) -> c, Tensor<n, a>, Tensor<n, b>) -> Tensor<n, c>
par_map  : Parallel a => ((a -> b), Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, b>
filter   : ((a -> Bool), Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
fold     : ((b, a) -> b, b, Tensor<1, a>) -> b
fold_left: same as fold
zip      : (Tensor<1, a>, Tensor<1, b>) -> Tensor<1, (a, b)>
zip_with : ((a, b) -> c, Tensor<n, a>, Tensor<n, b>) -> Tensor<n, c>
enumerate: Tensor<1, a> -> Tensor<1, (Int, a)>
range    : (Int, Int) -> Tensor<1, Int>
range    : (Int, Int, Int) -> Tensor<1, Int>
take     : (Int, Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
drop     : (Int, Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
reverse  : Tensor<1, a> -> Tensor<1, a>
reverse  : Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<n, a>                  # n ≥ 2: reverses the first axis
reverse_axis : (Tensor<n, a>, Int) -> Tensor<n, a>       # n ≥ 1: reverses the given axis
filter   : ((Tensor<n-1, a> -> Bool), Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>   # n ≥ 2: the predicate sees one row
take     : (Int, Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>           # n ≥ 2: takes the first rows
drop     : (Int, Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>
concat   : (Tensor<n, a>, Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>  # n ≥ 2: stacks along the first axis
any      : ((Tensor<n-1, a> -> Bool), Tensor<n, a>) -> Bool
all      : ((Tensor<n-1, a> -> Bool), Tensor<n, a>) -> Bool
find     : ((Tensor<n-1, a> -> Bool), Tensor<n, a>) -> Option<Tensor<n-1, a>>
first    : Tensor<n, a> -> Option<Tensor<n-1, a>>
last     : Tensor<n, a> -> Option<Tensor<n-1, a>>
sort     : TotalOrd a => Tensor<1, a> -> Tensor<1, a>
sort     : TotalOrd a => Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<n, a>      # n ≥ 2: rows in lexicographic order
sort_by  : ((a, a) -> Ordering, Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
sort_by  : ((Tensor<n-1, a>, Tensor<n-1, a>) -> Ordering, Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>
sort_by_key : TotalOrd k => ((a -> k), Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
sort_by_key : TotalOrd k => ((Tensor<n-1, a> -> k), Tensor<n, a>) -> Tensor<n, a>
sort_float : (Tensor<1, Float>, Bool) -> Tensor<1, Float>   # NaN last/first
sort_float : (Tensor<n, Float>, Bool) -> Tensor<n, Float>   # n ≥ 2: rows in lexicographic order
sort_axis : TotalOrd a => (Tensor<n, a>, Int) -> Tensor<n, a>   # sorts every 1-D slice along one axis
argsort  : TotalOrd a => Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<1, Int>        # permutation of a stable sort (row indices)
fold     : ((b, Tensor<n-1, a>) -> b, b, Tensor<n, a>) -> b    # n ≥ 2: the accumulator sees one row
zip      : (Tensor<n, a>, Tensor<m, b>) -> Tensor<1, (Tensor<n-1, a>, Tensor<m-1, b>)>   # pairs row by row
enumerate : Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<1, (Int, Tensor<n-1, a>)>    # n ≥ 2: index and row
concat   : (Tensor<1, a>, Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, a>
flatten  : Tensor<n, Tensor<m, a>> -> Tensor<n+m, a>   # regular nesting only
any      : ((a -> Bool), Tensor<1, a>) -> Bool
all      : ((a -> Bool), Tensor<1, a>) -> Bool
find     : ((a -> Bool), Tensor<1, a>) -> Option<a>
sum      : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<ρ,d>
prod     : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,1>> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
mean     : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d>
length   : Tensor<n, a> -> Int
shape    : Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<1, Int>
rank     : Tensor<n, a> -> Int
first    : Tensor<1, a> -> Option<a>
last     : Tensor<1, a> -> Option<a>
cumsum   : Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>>
cumprod  : Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,1>> -> Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,1>>
argmax   : Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Int      # index of the first maximum
argmin   : Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Int

Any rank. Operations written Tensor<n, …> are typed for every rank n: sum, prod, mean, var, std, norm, normalize, map, map2/zip_with (same shape), flatten (rank ≥ 2), transpose, the keepdims form of sum(a, axis), the whole FFT family of §8.8, and the sequence operations below. Rank is not a first-class type parameter, so these signatures are templates over the rank: the type table lists instances for ranks 1 to 4 (builtins::schemes, used for hovers and first-class use), and the checker instantiates the template at the rank of the actual argument. An argument whose rank matches no instance is E0302, with expected and actual types. first/last give None on an empty tensor; argmax/argmin on an empty tensor are E0802; NaN never wins, and ties take the lowest index.

Sequence operations act on the first axis. A tensor of rank n ≥ 2 is a sequence of rank-n−1 slices along the first axis ("rows"); Tensor<0, a> is a, so a "row" of a rank-1 tensor is an element.

  • reverse reverses the first axis and keeps the shape (to flatten, write reverse(flatten(m))). reverse_axis(t, k) reverses axis k (negative counts from the end, out of range E0802). On f64 tensors both are views and do not copy the buffer (§9.5).
  • filter(p, m): p takes a row (rank n−1); the result keeps rank n; rejecting everything gives a tensor of the same rank with first-axis length 0. any/all/find test rows one by one; find, first and last return Option<Tensor<n-1, a>>.
  • take(k, m)/drop(k, m) select rows; concat(a, b) stacks along the first axis and the other axes MUST agree (otherwise E0802).
  • fold(f, init, m)/fold_left: f(acc, row) runs from the first row to the last.
  • zip(a, b) pairs rows, truncating to the shorter, giving Tensor<1, (row-or-element, row-or-element)> (the operands may have different ranks); enumerate(m) gives a sequence of records with fields index and value.
  • sort(m) sorts rows lexicographically and stably, keeping the shape; the element type needs TotalOrd (use sort_float for Float). The comparison of sort_by(cmp, m) takes two rows; sort_by_key(f, m) sorts stably by the TotalOrd key f(row) (for rank 1, f takes an element); sort_float(m, nan_last) is sort with NaN first or last; argsort(m) returns the stable-sort permutation as Tensor<1, Int> (element indices for rank 1, row indices for rank ≥ 2); sort_axis(t, k) sorts every 1-D slice along axis k, keeping the shape.
  • Element-wise operations are unchanged: map/map2/zip_with act element by element and keep the shape; sum/prod/mean/var/std/norm/normalize reduce over all elements; cumsum/cumprod/argmax/argmin are defined for rank 1 only. For element-wise fold/sort/zip, flatten first.

length is overloaded on String and Tensor and resolved by the type class Sized.

par_map(f, xs) maps in parallel; the result order matches the input (the thread count never changes the result). It requires Parallel a: Int, Float[d] and their tensors can run in parallel; symbolic Real cannot (§3.8.4, §6.10). The element type is fixed at the call site, so the constraint is checked there rather than as a global instance. par_tabulate, par_reduce and rng are in the same family; par_reduce block boundaries depend only on the length (§9.7.2).

8.1.5.1 Sets

set_of       : TotalOrd a => Tensor<1, a> -> Set<a>   # deduplicated, ordered by total_compare
elements     : Set<a> -> Tensor<1, a>                 # ascending
length       : Set<a> -> Int                          # third overload of length
member       : (a, Set<a>) -> Bool                    # O(log n)
set_insert   : (Set<a>, a) -> Set<a>
set_remove   : (Set<a>, a) -> Set<a>
union        : (Set<a>, Set<a>) -> Set<a>
intersection : (Set<a>, Set<a>) -> Set<a>
difference   : (Set<a>, Set<a>) -> Set<a>
is_subset    : (Set<a>, Set<a>) -> Bool

Set<a> (§3.2, §6.6) is a persistent ordered set and requires TotalOrd a (Set<Float> is E0310). The representation is canonical — elements ascending by total_compare and unique — so == on sets is set equality regardless of insertion order; show prints {1, 2, 3} and repr prints set_of([1, 2, 3]). Set operations are linear merges and member is a binary search. Nothing is updated in place: set_insert returns a new set and the old value is unchanged (§0.4, principle 3).

8.1.6 Option / Result

Some     : a -> Option<a>
None     : Option<a>
Ok       : a -> Result<a, e>
Err      : e -> Result<a, e>
map      : ((a -> b), Option<a>) -> Option<b>
and_then : ((a -> Option<b>), Option<a>) -> Option<b>
unwrap_or: (Option<a>, a) -> a
is_some  : Option<a> -> Bool
is_ok    : Result<a, e> -> Bool
is_err   : Result<a, e> -> Bool
ok       : Result<a, e> -> Option<a>
ok_or    : (Option<a>, e) -> Result<a, e>
map      : ((a -> b), Result<a, e>) -> Result<b, e>
and_then : ((a -> Result<b, e>), Result<a, e>) -> Result<b, e>
unwrap_or: (Result<a, e>, a) -> a
is_none  : Option<a> -> Bool
map_err  : ((e -> f), Result<a, e>) -> Result<a, f>

? is syntax, not a function.

8.1.7 Display

show     : Show a => a -> String
repr     : Show a => a -> String
to_latex : Show a => a -> String
to_typst : Show a => a -> String
print    : Show a => a -> IO Unit
println  : Show a => a -> IO Unit
eprintln : Show a => a -> IO Unit

8.1.8 Program arguments

args : () -> Tensor<1, String>

args() returns the argument vector the host passes to the program, containing only the arguments after -- in aevia run file -- a b (-- itself is not an argument). With no arguments it is the empty tensor (length(args()) == 0, printed []). Arguments keep their original strings in order, with no shell parsing (quoting, spaces and globbing are the shell's business); the value does not change during the process, and repeated calls return the same vector. The type is Tensor<1, String>, so it can be mapped, indexed and measured.

8.2 Math

pi       : Real[1]
e        : Real[1]
i        : Complex[1]
oo       : Real[1]
nan      : Float[1]

oo is Real[1], not dimension-polymorphic; oo m is Real[L]. -oo comes from unary minus. nan is Float[1]. The semantics of infinity are in §6.3.4: which boundary values fold (ln(0)=-oo, ln(oo)=oo, exp(oo)=oo, exp(-oo)=0), which + - * / ^ expressions containing oo stay symbolic (the exact phase invents no NaN), and how the Float phase gives inf/nan per IEEE. This chapter declares only the types and dimensions of oo and nan.

8.2.1 Elementary functions

sqrt     : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d^(1/2)>        # ρ ∈ {Real,Float}
cbrt     : Scalar<ρ,d> -> Scalar<ρ,d^(1/3)>        # ρ ∈ {Real,Float}
exp      : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
ln       : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
log      : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>              # natural logarithm
log2     : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
log10    : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
sin cos tan          : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
asin acos atan        : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
atan2                : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,1>
sinh cosh tanh        : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
asinh acosh atanh     : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
hypot                : (Scalar<ρ,d>, Scalar<ρ,d>) -> Scalar<ρ,d>

8.2.2 Special functions

gamma        : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>
lgamma       : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>

fresnel_s fresnel_c : Scalar<ρ,1> -> Scalar<ρ,1>   # S(x)=∫₀ˣ sin(πt²/2)dt, C likewise (DLMF 7.2.7)

Arguments of special functions MUST be dimensionless; results are dimensionless.

8.3 Function utilities

curry    : ((a, b) -> c) -> (a -> (b -> c))
uncurry  : (a -> (b -> c)) -> ((a, b) -> c)
partial  : ((a, b) -> c, a) -> (b -> c)

partial(f, a) is the function form of f(a, _).

8.4 LinearAlgebra

dot      : (Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>>, Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,e>>) -> Scalar<ρ,d*e>
matmul   : (Tensor<2, Scalar<ρ,d>>, Tensor<2, Scalar<ρ,e>>) -> Tensor<2, Scalar<ρ,d*e>>

transpose: Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<n, a>          # reverses the axis order

         -> Result<Tensor<1, Scalar<div_rep(ρ),e/d>>, LinAlgError>

         -> Result<Tensor<1, Scalar<div_rep(ρ),e/d>>, LinAlgError>

flatten  : Tensor<n, a> -> Tensor<1, a>

map_indexed : ((Int, a) -> b, Tensor<1, a>) -> Tensor<1, b>
approx_map : ((Real[d] -> Real[e]), Tensor<1, Real[d]>)
           -> Result<Tensor<1, Float[e]>, EvalError>
sum      : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<ρ,d>
sum      : (Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>>, Int) -> Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>>   # keepdims
mean     : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d>
var      : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d^2>
std      : Tensor<n, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Scalar<div_rep(ρ),d>

On tensors of rank ≥ 2, transpose reverses the axis order ([a, b, c] of rank 3 becomes [c, b, a], and refined shapes reverse likewise); on rank 1 it is E0302 (a vector has no second axis, and the hint says so). On rank ≥ 2, norm is the norm over all elements (p = 2 is the Frobenius norm), and var/std/mean/sum/prod reduce over all elements (§8.1.5).

In zeros<Float>(3, 4), n=2; zeros<Int> fixes the element type. zeros/ones/fill are built-in variadic: n is the number of Int arguments. They can be imported from Tensor or LinearAlgebra and re-exported through value aliases; the type argument may be given before or at the call (let z = zeros<Int>; z(2, 3)). A local definition with the same name shadows the built-in as usual, and constructors passed through a generic identity function keep their identity. zeros and ones of the same element type can be branches of one conditional expression (the operation is chosen at run time by the actual value); fill is a separate signature family and different specialisations cannot be mixed. A higher-order parameter with a fixed signature cannot take a variadic constructor directly; write a fixed-arity lambda such as fn (n) => zeros(n). In reshape<[n1, ..., nk]>(a) the target shape is explicit; the element count is checked at compile time when shapes are known, otherwise at run time. reshape_dyn(a, shape) takes a run-time shape vector and returns a Result.

Shape branching: refine_shape. refine_shape<[3, ?]>(a) checks at run time whether each extent of a equals the literal written in the type argument (? matches any extent); on a match it returns Some(b) (b : Tensor<[3, ?], τ>), otherwise None. The rank is checked at compile time (a mismatch is E0302). So shape-dependent branching is a match on the Option, and in each branch b already carries concrete extents in its type, which later matrix products, indexing and reshape check statically:

match refine_shape<[3, 3]>(m) with
| Some(sq) -> sq @ sq            # sq : Tensor<[3, 3], Float>, the multiplication shapes are statically known
| None -> m

An extent MUST be a literal or ?: type-level variables have no run-time value, so refine_shape<[n]>(a) is E0304 (refining a variable extent can only be solved from equations in a signature, not by a run-time test).

@ dispatches on rank: A @ B (2×2), A @ x (2×1), x @ A (1×2), x @ y (1×1 dot product). matmul is 2×2 only; matvec/vecmat/dot are the explicit forms.

det accepts only dimensionless matrices; use det_unit for dimensioned ones:

det_unit : (Tensor<2, Scalar<ρ,d>>, Unit[d]) -> Dyn<ρ>

type LinAlgError = | Singular | NotConverged | NotPositiveDefinite | ShapeMismatch

8.4.1 Sparse

type Sparse<F, shape, τ>

from_dense<F> : Tensor<shape, τ> -> Sparse<F, shape, τ>
to_dense      : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> Tensor<shape, τ>
convert<G>    : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> Sparse<G, shape, τ>
to_bcsr       : (Sparse<F, [m,n], τ>, Int, Int) -> Sparse<Bcsr, [m,n], τ>
block_size    : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> (Int, Int)
nnz           : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> Int
density       : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> Float
format_of     : Sparse<F, shape, τ> -> String
sparse_coo    : (Tensor<1, Int>, Tensor<2, Int>, Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>>)
              -> Sparse<Coo, shape, Scalar<ρ,d>>
sparse_csr    : (Int, Int, Tensor<1, Int>, Tensor<1, Int>, Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>>)
              -> Sparse<Csr, [m,n], Scalar<ρ,d>>
speye<F>      : Int -> Sparse<F, [n,n], Float>
spdiag<F>     : Tensor<1, Scalar<ρ,d>> -> Sparse<F, [n,n], Scalar<ρ,d>>
